2. Product Background  
2.1 Context 
Solar energy systems using lithium-ion batteries are increasingly prevalent across industrial and 
residential sectors. Lithium-ion batteries require continuous monitoring of critical technical 
parameters such as voltage (V), current (A), temperature (T), state of charge (SOC%), and state of 
health (SOH%) to ensure safe and efficient operation. 
Currently, operators managing solar battery systems lack an integrated platform capable of: 
- Monitoring battery status in real time across multiple assets 
- Managing incidents through a standardized workflow with SLA guarantees 
- Predicting early battery degradation using AI to enable proactive preventive maintenance 
scheduling and provide repair recommendations. 
The GSU26SE55 project builds the Solar Lithium-ion Battery Maintenance Management System — an 
integrated platform consisting of three core components:

2.2 Proposed Solutions 
● P1 — Battery condition is only discovered after the failure 
Battery systems operate continuously, but operators have no centralized monitoring platform. 
Failures are only discovered after obvious damage has occurred, leading to power outages or serious 
safety hazards such as thermal runaway and fire. 
=> Solution: BatteryService with TimescaleDB hypertable ingests sensor readings every 30 seconds; 
the Mobile App displays real-time data via REST/SSE. 
● P2 — Incident handling follows no defined process and no deadline 
When an incident occurs, there is no ticketing system, no SLA, and no clear role-based accountability. 
Operators have no visibility into who is handling an issue, its current progress, or whether it will be 
resolved on time. 
=> Solution: TicketService with a complete 8-state machine (Open, Pending, InProgress, Request, 
ReAssign, Completed, Closed, ClosedRejected), an automated SLA timer, and tier-based Staff 
assignment. 
 
● P3 — Static thresholds bury operators in false alarms 
Alerts based on simple static thresholds generate excessive false positives (sensor noise) or miss real 
anomalies. The system cannot distinguish genuine faults from hardware noise. 
11 
 
=> Solution: Isolation Forest anomaly detection combined with frequency-based noise suppression 
— an alert is raised only after at least 5 threshold breaches within a 24-hour window, and the 
damping is bypassed for environmental incidents and for critical overheat. 
 
● P4 — Degradation stays invisible until the pack dies 
Without a tool to predict SOH over time, operators cannot schedule preventive maintenance. Sudden 
battery failure leads to costly emergency replacements. 
=> Solution: a Mamba state-space model (two Mamba blocks with FiLM conditioning and attention 
pooling, 73,729 parameters) trained on the NASA Ames Battery Dataset and extended with the 
Severson 2019 LFP and SNL datasets; classifies battery state as Normal / Degrading / Failed and feeds 
SOH into the alert pipeline. It outperforms a CNN-LSTM baseline on the same protocol (MAE 1.63% 
vs 4.90%). 
● P5 — Every recurring fault is solved again from scratch 
Each time a recurring incident occurs, it must be resolved from scratch. Technical experience is not 
retained, and new Staff members have no reference documentation. 
=> Solution: a Guide article entity with a four-state lifecycle (Draft, PendingReview, Published, 
Archived) and full version history; Staff attach the articles they consulted to the ticket, and a 
Manager or Admin reviews each article before it is published. 
● P6 — Actions on the system cannot be traced back to anyone 
There is no mechanism to track who performed what action, when, and why. The system cannot 
meet audit and traceability requirements. 
=> Solution: TicketActivity entity records all critical operations (actor + timestamp + old/new value + 
reason); AuditLog in AuthService tracks all admin-level changes. 
 
3. Existing Systems  
Market Analysis: The lithium-ion battery management market    currently comprises three distinct 
categories, each serving a specific need but none fully addressing the operational requirements of 
solar SME operators: 
-  Group 1 — Pure Monitoring: Real-time battery status monitoring with no AI prediction and 
no incident management workflow. 
- Group 2 — Enterprise APM/CMMS: Full maintenance workflows but extremely high 
operating costs, designed for organizations with 1,000+ users. 
-  Group 3 — AI Battery Analytics: AI-powered analytics for batteries but targeting grid-scale 
deployments (tens of GWh), unsuitable for SME scale. 
3.1 Victron Energy VRM Portal 
Description: A free web portal bundled with Victron hardware (GX devices) that enables real-time 
monitoring of SOC, voltage, and temperature. Widely used in off-grid and hybrid solar segments, 
particularly among residential and small business users.  
- System Actors: Individual homeowners, Small business operators, Solar installers. 
 
- Features: Real-time monitoring dashboard, historical data storage, mobile app support, basic 
threshold alerts, Solar Production Forecast (AI-based for solar yield — not battery SOH). 
 
Pros: 
-  Completely free when used with Victron GX hardware 
- Long-term historical data storage with mobile app support 
- Large and active user community for support 
12 
 
Cons: 
- No AI-based SOH prediction: Victron engineers have officially confirmed at the Victron 
Community Forum that calculating SOH automatically is nearly impossible for most systems. 
Users have no visibility into remaining battery lifetime. 
-  No incident management workflow: No ticketing system, no SLA, no Staff assignment 
mechanism. 
-  Hardware lock-in: Functions exclusively within the Victron ecosystem; incompatible with 
third-party hardware. 
-  No intelligent anomaly detection: Alerts rely solely on static thresholds without the ability to 
detect complex anomalies such as cell imbalance or degradation patterns. 
 
3.2 IBM Maximo Application Suite (Energy & Utilities) 
 
Description: 
The world's leading Enterprise Asset Performance Management (APM) platform, with a Renewables 
module specifically for wind, solar, and battery storage. Includes full CMMS capabilities: work orders, 
preventive maintenance scheduling, asset hierarchy management, and AI-driven predictive 
maintenance analytics. 
- System Actors: Enterprise asset managers, Maintenance engineers, Operations directors, IT 
administrators. 
- Features: Full CMMS with automated work order workflows, AI and ML for predictive 
maintenance on renewable energy assets, integration with ERP, GIS, and EAM systems. 
 
Pros: 
- Comprehensive CMMS with automated maintenance workflows and work order 
management 
- AI and ML capabilities for predictive maintenance on renewable energy assets 
-  Deep integration with enterprise systems (SAP, Oracle, GIS) 
 
Cons: 
- Prohibitive cost: The Essentials tier is priced at approximately $3,150 USD/month for up to 
25 users. The 5-year total cost of ownership for 500 users is estimated at $4M–$7.5M. 
-  Complex implementation: Average deployment time is 18–24 months and requires a 
dedicated team of specialist consultants. 
- Poor mobile adoption: Field adoption rates are only 30–40% due to complex UX. Creating a 
single work order requires approximately 15 steps. 
- Not specialized for lithium-ion SME: Lacks a sequence model specifically tuned for 
cycle-based SOH prediction; does not support ITIL SLA P1/P2/P3 customized by an Impact x 
Urgency matrix for battery incidents. 
-  Over-engineered for SME scale: SME organizations typically use fewer than 20% of Maximo's 
available features. 
 
3.3 ACCURE Battery Intelligence 
 
13 
 
Description: A cloud-native AI platform specialized in monitoring large-scale lithium-ion battery 
fleets (BESS), supporting over 18 GWh deployed globally. Provides SOC accuracy optimization (±2%), 
degradation analysis, anomaly detection, and internal task management. 
- System Actors: Grid-scale utility operators, BESS asset managers, Energy storage engineers. 
- Features: AI-native large-scale BESS fleet monitoring, predictive analytics for early failure 
detection, SOC accuracy optimization (±2%), degradation trend analysis over time. 
 
Pros: 
- AI-native platform for large-scale BESS fleet monitoring — closest competitor to GSU26SE55 
in AI battery analytics capability 
- Strong predictive analytics for early failure detection in lithium-ion batteries 
- High SOC accuracy (±2%) with long-term degradation trend analysis 
 
Cons: 
-  Wrong market segment: Targets grid-scale utilities (18+ GWh) — not SME commercial or 
residential solar (5–500 kWh range). 
-  No ITIL ticketing workflow: No ticket state machine, no SLA P1/P2/P3, no escalation flow, no 
skill-based Staff assignment. 
-  No Customer mobile app: Only an internal operator portal — battery system owners have no 
personal monitoring channel. 
- Opaque enterprise pricing: High barrier for SME operators who want to evaluate the 
platform before commitment. 
 
 
 
 
4. Business Opportunity  
There is a clear and unaddressed gap in the market for an integrated platform that combines 
real-time battery monitoring, ITIL-compliant incident management, and AI-driven predictive analytics 
into a single, accessible solution tailored for SME solar operators. 
 
Existing solutions fall into one of three gaps: pure monitoring tools (Victron VRM) that lack AI 
prediction and incident workflows; enterprise CMMS platforms (IBM Maximo) that are prohibitively 
expensive and complex for SMEs; and large-scale AI analytics platforms (ACCURE) that target 
grid-scale utilities and are inaccessible to small and medium operators. 
 
By building GSU26SE55, the team addresses this gap directly. The platform enables operators to 
monitor battery health in real time via a mobile app, manage incidents end-to-end with automated 
SLA enforcement and Staff assignment, predict battery degradation early using a Mamba state-space 
model to avoid costly emergency replacements, and maintain a structured guide that accumulates 
institutional expertise over time. 
 
The smart alert system — combining Isolation Forest anomaly detection with frequency-based noise 
suppression — significantly reduces false positives while ensuring real faults are never missed. The 
ITIL-compliant ticket lifecycle with P1/P2/P3 SLA tiers provides a structured process that is currently 
absent from every competing solution in this market segment. Taken together, these capabilities 
14 
 
represent a defensible and commercially relevant product that directly addresses the pain points of 
the growing solar energy SME market. 
 
5. Software Product Vision  
For solar system operators and maintenance teams who struggle with fragmented monitoring tools, 
reactive incident handling, and accelerating battery degradation, the Solar Lithium-ion Battery 
Maintenance Management System (GSU26SE55) is an integrated web and mobile platform that 
consolidates real-time battery monitoring, ITIL-compliant incident ticketing, and AI-driven SOH 
prediction into a single, role-based interface. 
Unlike existing platforms that offer only raw monitoring data without predictive intelligence (Victron 
VRM), or enterprise-grade CMMS solutions that are prohibitively expensive and complex for SME 
operators (IBM Maximo), or AI analytics platforms designed exclusively for grid-scale utilities 
(ACCURE), this product integrates a Mamba state-space model for battery lifetime prediction, an 
Isolation Forest anomaly detector with frequency-based noise suppression, and a full 8-state ticket 
lifecycle with automated SLA enforcement and escalation. 
Operators using GSU26SE55 will detect battery anomalies before failure occurs, resolve incidents 
through a structured and accountable process, prevent emergency replacements through early 
degradation warnings, and accumulate institutional maintenance knowledge — all through a 
platform purpose-built for the scale and budget of SME solar operations. 
6. Project Scope & Limitations  
6.1 Major Features  
Functional requirement  
Customer (Mobile App) Features: 
● FR-01: Real-time battery dashboard displaying Voltage, Current, Temperature, SOC% 
and SOH% for all owned assets, together with the rolling peak charge current and 
peak discharge current over two fixed windows (the last hour and the current day). 
● FR-02: Historical trend charts for 7-day and 30-day periods. Each time bucket carries 
the average, minimum and maximum voltage and temperature, and the minimum, 
average and peak charge current and discharge current reported separately by 
direction, with the sample count per direction. 
● FR-03: Push notifications when battery parameters exceed configured alert 
thresholds. 
● FR-04: Management of multiple Battery Assets (multiple devices under one account). 
● FR-05: Manual incident ticket creation with issue type selection, description, and 
media attachments (up to 5 files). 
● FR-06: Incident ticket tracking with real-time status updates and activity timeline. 
● FR-07: Comment and reply thread with assigned Staff for additional information 
exchange. 
● FR-08: Ticket reopen within 7 days if the resolution is unsatisfactory. 
● FR-09: Post-resolution rating (1–5 stars) for each closed ticket. 
 
Admin Features:  
● FR-10: Full user account lifecycle management — create, update, and deactivate 
accounts by role (Admin / Manager / Staff / Customer). 
15 
 
● FR-11: Battery Type configuration (LFP, NMC, NCA, etc.) and ThresholdConfig 
management per battery type. 
● FR-12: Battery Asset registry — serial number, battery type, owner, installation date, 
warranty, and location. 
● FR-13: SLA rule definition — P1 Critical (4h), P2 High (24h), P3 Normal (72h) with 
corresponding Staff tier assignments; Urgent is the highest level and is tracked 
without an SLA timer. 
● FR-14: System-wide audit log viewing and CSV export. 
 
Manager Features (Web App): 
● FR-15: Ticket triage queue. Priority is derived from the ITIL Impact x Urgency matrix 
recorded by the Manager at intake. AI assists separately by verifying customer-raised 
tickets (Pending / Legitimate / Suspicious / Skipped) and by suggesting a handler and 
relevant guide articles. 
●  FR-16: Sensor data, Customer-uploaded media, and dependency report review 
before triage. 
● FR-17: Priority adjustment (with mandatory reason logged to Activity Timeline) and 
tier-based Staff assignment. 
● FR-18: Real-time SLA countdown per ticket. 
● FR-19: Maintenance Log and Wiki review — Approve or Reject (with note returned to 
Staff). 
● FR-20: Ticket escalation — reassign to a higher-tier Staff when needed. 
● FR-21: KPI dashboard — Open / Overdue / SLA breach rate; Staff performance, SLA 
compliance, and CSAT reports. 
Staff Features (Web App/Mobile App): 
● FR-22: New ticket notifications with SLA countdown displayed by color (green / 
yellow / red). 
● FR-23: Guide search by incident type. 
● FR-24: Customer comment and reply thread management. 
● FR-25: Ticket hold (WAITING_CUSTOMER / WAITING_PARTS) with SLA pause and 
mandatory reason. 
● FR-26: Maintenance Log entry — root cause and resolution actions performed. 
● FR-27: Wiki article creation or update (mandatory before marking Resolved). 
● FR-28: Mark Resolved or proactively request escalation at 2/3 SLA elapsed. 
 
System Features: 
● FR-29: Background sensor scan every 30 seconds; comparison against 
ThresholdConfig per Battery Type. 
● FR-30: Detection of 17 anomaly types across pin-level (overheat, over/under-voltage, 
low SOC, rapid discharge, abnormal charging, device offline, SOH degradation), 
under-temperature, ambient, Tier-2 battery health (internal resistance, cell 
imbalance), site-level environmental, cross-source BMS-versus-IoT mismatch, and 
IoT data-integrity categories. 
● FR-31: Frequency-based noise suppression — an alert is created only after at least 5 
threshold breaches within a 24-hour window; the damping is bypassed for 
environmental incidents and for critical overheat. 
16 
 
● FR-32: Alert deduplication — same asset + anomaly type + time window merged into 
existing alert. 
● FR-33: Dependency check — detect cascading battery failure impact; escalate 
priority automatically. 
● FR-34: Auto-create ticket for Critical alerts with dependency (no Customer action 
required). 
● FR-35: Mamba state-space SOH prediction trained on the NASA Ames Battery 
Dataset (24 training cells, validation on B0046, held-out test on B0048), extended 
with the Severson 2019 LFP and SNL datasets. 
● FR-36: Isolation Forest anomaly detection (high recall priority — safety-critical 
context). 
● FR-37: Battery state classification — Normal (SOH >= 90%) / Degrading (80-90%) / 
Failed (< 80%). The API additionally returns a two-stage health stage, Healthy or End 
Of Life, split at the 80% end-of-life threshold. 
● FR-38: SLA timer lifecycle — start on assignment, pause/resume with controlled 
reason logging, warning at 80%, auto-escalate at 2/3 elapsed, breach action at 100%. 
● FR-39: Real-time notifications delivered over SignalR and rendered by a native 
Android notification module with three channels (critical alerts, default alerts, chat 
messages); email notifications for account invitation, OTP and alert digest; SMS 
through the registered gateway device. 
● Cascade Risk & Protective Control: 
● FR-40: Electrical topology configuration per battery asset (Independent, Series 
String, Parallel Bank, Series-Parallel) - Admin only; changing it recomputes the 
cascade risk of that asset immediately. 
● FR-41: Cascade risk scoring, recomputed on a background cycle from three additive 
rules - the electrical topology of the asset, the number of sibling assets at the same 
site holding an open alert within the last hour, and a critical overheat alert on the 
asset itself. The score is capped at 1.0 and classified Low (< 0.5), Medium (0.5-0.7) 
and High (>= 0.7). 
● FR-42: When an asset crosses into High cascade risk the platform escalates the 
related ticket to P1 Critical and recomputes its SLA deadline; if no such ticket exists it 
opens a site-scope P1 incident ticket automatically. 
● FR-43: High cascade risk notifies every active Manager and Admin in-app, by push 
and by email, debounced to one notification per asset per 30 minutes. 
● FR-44: Site-level cascade risk summary - asset counts per level, the maximum score 
at the site, and the list of high-risk assets (Admin and Manager). 
● FR-45: Remote BMS switch - charge and discharge can each be enabled or disabled 
independently to isolate a pack in an emergency. The request is issued as an MQTT 
downlink command to the site gateway and written to the BMS over Modbus. A 
Customer may operate only the batteries they own. 
● FR-46: Every switch command is persisted with its command id, issuing account and 
status (Pending, Ok, Failed, Rejected, Unknown, TimedOut). A second pending 
command for the same target is refused, the gateway acknowledges with the 
readback state, and a mismatch between the requested and the applied state is 
reported as a failure. 
● FR-47: The client requires an explicit additional confirmation before sending a switch 
command while the asset is at High cascade risk. 
● Authentication & Account (Web + Mobile): 
● FR-48: Two-factor authentication - TOTP authenticator app, SMS code, single-use 
backup codes, or approval from an already trusted device. 
17 
 
● FR-49: Google OAuth 2.0 sign-in - callback route on the web, native Google Sign-In 
SDK on mobile. 
● FR-50: Invitation flow for internal accounts; the invited user sets a password and 
activates the account from the invitation link. 
● FR-51: Account reactivation after lockout caused by repeated failed sign-in attempts. 
● FR-52: Session and trusted device management - list and revoke active sessions and 
registered devices. 
● FR-53: Role and permission matrix administration. Route access is decided by role 
alone; permission codes control which actions appear inside a screen. 
● Site, Asset & IoT Device Management: 
● FR-54: Site management - installation site with GPS coordinates and installed 
capacity; site detail shows the assets, environmental readings and cascade risk. 
● FR-55: IoT device registry - register a gateway, issue and rotate its API key, and 
monitor its online or offline state. 
● FR-56: Device heartbeat every 60 seconds; a gateway whose heartbeat stops beyond 
the configured window is marked offline and raises a device alert. 
● FR-57: Firmware release catalogue and over-the-air deployment to selected devices, 
with the from/to version and outcome tracked per device. 
● FR-58: Sensor calibration records with offset and scale factor, and a warning raised 
before a calibration expires. 
● Alerts & Environmental Monitoring: 
● FR-59: Site-level alert aggregation - five or more assets raising the same anomaly at 
one site inside the window merge into a single site alert. 
● FR-60: Alert acknowledgement escalation ladder - 15 minutes to Staff, 30 minutes to 
Manager with a P1 ticket opened automatically. 
● FR-61: Silence rules that suppress alerts for a known issue under repair, scoped to an 
asset, a site or a battery type. 
● FR-62: Environmental incident recording (smoke, water ingress) - more severe than a 
threshold alert and never closed automatically. 
● FR-63: Per-site ambient temperature and humidity thresholds; ambient data comes 
from site sensors, or from the Open-Meteo API where a site has no sensor. 
● Ticket Chat & Collaboration: 
● FR-64: Real-time ticket chat with attachments, emoji reactions, mentions and read 
receipts, plus a cross-ticket inbox and a mentions list. 
● FR-65: Separate public and internal threads on the same ticket; internal messages 
are never visible to the Customer. 
● FR-66: On-demand message translation, with technical terms and identifiers masked 
before translation and restored afterwards. 
● FR-67: Voice messages with transcription, and AI assistance that drafts a reply or 
summarises a long thread - every suggestion is reviewed before it is sent. 
● FR-68: Content-safety pipeline on every message - personally identifiable 
information detection, profanity filtering and spam scoring. 
● Guide & Blog: 
● FR-69: Guide articles with a four-state lifecycle (Draft, PendingReview, Published, 
Archived), version history and side-by-side version comparison. 
● FR-70: AI-assisted recommendation of relevant guide articles on a ticket, with a 
match score and the reason for the match. 
● FR-71: Customer-facing blog post generated from a published article, reviewed by a 
Manager before publication. 
● Notification & Multi-Channel Delivery: 
18 
 
● FR-72: Per-channel and per-event notification preferences, registered device 
management, and unsubscribe. 
● FR-73: Broadcast notification composed by Admin and delivered to selected users or 
groups over the enabled channels. 
● Energy & Fleet Analytics: 
● FR-74: Charge and discharge session detection with energy, start and end state of 
charge, and peak power. 
● FR-75: Cycle logging with depth of discharge, round-trip efficiency and a stress score 
fed back to the health model. 
● Platform & Cross-Service Consistency: 
● FR-76: Transactional outbox - a state change and the events it implies are committed 
in one transaction and published to the broker afterwards. 
● FR-77: Saga orchestration with explicit compensation for workflows that span 
services, with the saga state inspectable from an admin screen. 
● FR-78: Audit aggregation - audit records emitted by every service collected into a 
single append-only, queryable trail. 
● FR-79: File access auditing and malware scanning; a file that fails the scan is deleted 
from storage and never reaches a record. 
● FR-80: Preventive maintenance scheduling - a maintenance schedule that falls due 
opens a P3 ticket linked to the asset and the schedule that produced it. 
 
6.2 Non-functional Requirements 
NFR-01 — Performance 
● API response time ≤ 500ms for 95% of requests under normal load 
● Sensor ingest cycle completes within 30 seconds across all active assets 
● Mobile App dashboard renders within 10 seconds on launch 
NFR-02 — Scalability 
● System supports minimum 100 concurrent Battery Assets per account 
● TimescaleDB hypertable designed for horizontal scaling as data volume grows 
NFR-03 — Availability 
● System uptime ≥ 99% during operating hours 
● If one microservice fails, remaining services continue operating — zero cascade 
failures 
NFR-04 — Security 
● All API endpoints protected by JWT Authentication + RBAC 
● OTP verification required for registration and password reset 
● Passwords stored using cryptographic hashing — no plain-text storage 
NFR-05 — Reliability 
● SLA timer guarantees 100% accuracy across all state transitions (start / pause / 
resume / breach) 
● Outbox + Inbox pattern ensures zero message loss on service restart 
● Alert deduplication prevents duplicate notifications for the same incident 
19 
 
NFR-06 — Usability 
● Customer can create a ticket in ≤ 3 steps on the Mobile App 
● SLA countdown displays color-coded status: green (safe) / yellow (warning) / red 
(critical) 
● Web App supports responsive layout at ≥ 1280px screen width 
NFR-07 — Auditability 
● All critical operations logged to TicketActivity with actor, timestamp, old/new value, 
and reason 
● Admin can export full audit log as CSV at any time 
NFR-08 — Maintainability 
● Backend follows Clean Architecture 4-layer pattern (Domain / Application / 
Infrastructure / Api) 
● CI/CD pipeline (Jenkins) runs build, lint, unit tests and security scanning on every pull 
request 
● All services containerized with Docker for consistent deployment and rollback 
NFR-09 — AI Model Quality 
● Anomaly detection (Isolation Forest) prioritizes high Recall — no real fault may be 
missed 
● SOH prediction (Mamba state-space model): target MAE < 2%, RMSE < 3% on the 
NASA Ames held-out test cell 
● AI inference latency < 100ms per request 
● Measured results: SOH prediction reaches MAE 1.63% and RMSE 2.09% on the 
held-out test cell B0048, meeting the target; across a 16-fold leave-one-battery-out 
evaluation the cross-battery figure is MAE 3.91% +/- 3.27% (median 2.22%). Anomaly 
detection is not yet held to a comparable numeric target: the NASA dataset carries 
no real fault annotations, so evaluation relies on a proxy label derived from the 
capacity fade rate, on which the shipped decision thresholds score F1 = 0.000 and a 
validation-tuned threshold reaches F1 = 0.342 on the test split. Measured inference 
latency is 51-59ms on average on a development CPU with the production weights. 
NFR-10 — Notification Reliability 
● Real-time notifications include retry logic and a background catch-up path on delivery 
failure 
● Email notifications (OTP, alerts) delivered within ≤ 60 seconds 
20 
 
 
6.3 Limitations & Exclusions 
● LI-1: No billing or payment processing for maintenance services is included in this version. 
● LI-2: Two AI capabilities are deliberately not surfaced in this release. The 
remaining-useful-life output is a research capability that no screen consumes, and the 
prescription endpoint is specified but not built — the backend holds the client interface only. 
No requirement in this document depends on either. 
● LI-3: Multi-tenant architecture (multiple independent organizations on one system) is not 
supported in this version. 
● LI-4: The Mobile App serves the Customer and Staff roles only. Admin and Manager accounts 
access the system through the Web App exclusively, and a Customer account cannot sign in 
to the Web App. 
● LI-5: The Mobile App requires an active internet connection for real-time telemetry and chat; 
only notification catch-up runs in the background while the app is suspended. Offline 
buffering with replay is implemented at the IoT gateway, not in the app. 
● LI-6: The AI model is trained offline on the NASA Ames Battery Dataset (24 training cells, 
validation on B0046, held-out test on B0048), extended with the Severson 2019 LFP and SNL 
cycling datasets, and is deployed as a versioned artefact. Technicians can mark a 
classification as correct, a false alarm or a missed fault, and that feedback is retained for 
model improvement, but retraining is performed manually - no automated retraining loop is 
in scope for this capstone release. 
● LI-7: The AI SOH prediction and anomaly detection outputs are analytical decision-support 
tools. They do not constitute professional engineering certification or serve as a replacement 
for qualified technical inspection. 
21 
 
● LI-8: Two of the seventeen anomaly types - high internal resistance and cell imbalance - are 
implemented in the detection rules and covered by unit tests, but cannot fire end-to-end in 
the current deployment: the gateway firmware does not yet publish the two corresponding 
measurements and no default threshold is seeded for them. They are specified here as 
designed behaviour, not as demonstrated capability. 
 
     
 
 
 
 
22

2. Management Approach  
2.1 Project Process  
The team adopts an Agile development methodology, enabling iterative delivery of working features 
and continuous refinement based on mentor and stakeholder feedback. Development is organized 
into 8 two-week sprints from 11/05/2026 to 31/08/2026, with a final documentation phase from 
01/09 to 06/09/2026. 
Key Components:  
Requirement Analysis  
● The team begins with a Minimum Viable Product (MVP) focused on the highest-priority 
features: real-time battery monitoring, ITIL ticket management, and AI anomaly detection.  
● Requirements are continuously refined during backlog grooming sessions in collaboration 
with the supervisor and team members. 
● All prioritization decisions consider technical feasibility (AI model constraints, IoT ingest rate 
limits), business value, and academic objectives, ensuring every sprint produces meaningful 
and demonstrable progress 
Project Design  
● The system architecture is established at the start of Sprint 1 with a complete SRS, ERD, and 
Architecture Diagram - covering the microservices structure (ApiGateway plus AuthService, 
BatteryService, TicketService, NotificationService, EmailService, SmsService, 
FileStorageService and AuditAggregatorService), the TimescaleDB hypertable schema for 
time-series data, and the AI module interface contract. 
● The UI/UX Mockup and API Contract (Swagger) are finalized and locked before FE/Mobile 
development begins in Sprint 2, preventing downstream blocking dependencies. 
Project Implementation (Development Iterations)  
● Development proceeds in two-week sprints, each focused on a defined set of features.  
● Each sprint ends with a working, integrated build.  
● During implementation, Frontend and Backend developers maintain close coordination using 
a locked API contract and mock server to eliminate sequential dependencies.  
● Progress is tracked via GitHub Issues with fields for Complexity, Priority, Sprint, and Status. 
Project Testing  
● Testing is integrated into every sprint rather than performed as a separate final phase.  
● Unit tests are written alongside feature code.  
28 
 
● Integration tests validate service interactions after each sprint.  
● System testing (Sprint 7) covers the full end-to-end platform including AI model evaluation.  
● User Acceptance Testing is performed with the supervisor at the end of each sprint to 
validate feature correctness against requirements. 
Project Deployment (Incremental Delivery)  
● The system is maintained in a continuously deployable state.  
● After each stable sprint, a working build is deployed to the staging environment, enabling 
early validation of real-time data connections, SLA automation, and AI inference 
performance.  
● The production Docker Compose deployment is finalized in Sprint 8. 
Project Maintenance 
● Issues identified after deployment — including AI model accuracy drift, edge cases in SLA 
logic, or notification delivery failures — are logged as GitHub Issues, triaged during sprint 
review, and scheduled for resolution in the following sprint.  
● Future enhancement items (preventive maintenance scheduling, multi-tenant support) are 
tracked in the backlog for post-capstone development. 
2.2 Quality Management  
The team integrates quality assurance throughout every sprint rather than treating it as a terminal 
phase. The approach emphasizes early defect prevention, peer review, and iterative testing. 
Defect Prevention  
Code Reviews  
● Peer Reviews: Every feature branch must pass peer code review before merging to dev. This 
is enforced via GitHub Pull Request workflow with required reviewer approval. Reviews focus 
on correctness, adherence to Clean Architecture, and consistency with the API contract. 
● Coding Standards: The team follows consistent coding standards across all services - Clean 
Architecture 4-layer (Domain / Application / Infrastructure / Api) for all .NET services; 
TanStack Query + React Hook Form + Zod for Frontend; Expo Router v6 for Mobile. This 
minimizes integration issues and reduces onboarding friction. 
Design Reviews  
● Approach: Design artifacts (ERD, architecture diagram, API contract, state diagrams) are 
reviewed at the start of each sprint. Any changes to the architecture or data model require 
team agreement and are reflected immediately in the Swagger contract and shared 
documentation. 
Requirements Reviews  
● Approach: Requirements are revisited during backlog grooming at the start of each sprint. The 
supervisor reviews and approves the sprint backlog before development begins. Any changes 
to feature scope or acceptance criteria are communicated to all team members and logged in 
the sprint planning notes. 
Testing  
29 
 
Unit Testing  
● Approach: Developers write unit tests alongside feature code using xUnit and Moq (.NET). 
Target: at least 70% line coverage across the Application and Domain layers, which hold the 
CQRS handlers and business rules. The CI pipeline (Jenkins) runs all unit tests on every push 
and blocks merges on test failure. 
Integration Testing  
● Approach: Integration tests validate cross-service interactions: JWT authentication flow, SLA 
timer pause/resume/breach scenarios, sensor ingest through to alert creation, and 
notification dispatch. Integration tests run in the CI pipeline against a Docker Compose test 
environment. 
System Testing  
● Approach: Sprint 7 is dedicated to comprehensive system testing: full E2E golden path testing 
for all user roles, AI model evaluation against the NASA Ames held-out test cell, performance 
baseline checks on the sensor ingest path, and a service-down check to validate graceful 
degradation. Load, stress and penetration testing are out of scope for this capstone - see 
section V.1 Scope of Testing. 
Acceptance Testing  
● Approach: The supervisor reviews the sprint demo at the end of each sprint to confirm that 
implemented features meet requirements and academic quality standards. Feedback from 
these sessions is logged and scheduled into the next sprint. 
Defect Tracking  
● Approach: All defects identified during development, testing, or review are logged as GitHub 
Issues with labels (bug, priority, sprint). Defects are discussed during daily standup or sprint 
review and resolved in the current or immediately following sprint. No known critical defect 
may remain open at sprint close.

6. Configuration Management  
6.1 Document Management  
Document tools: Google Docs, Google Sheets, Figma (UI/UX Mockup) 
File management: Google Drive (shared team folder) 
Naming convention: [GSU26SE55]_[DocumentType]_[Version].ext 
6.2 Source Code Management 
Primary tool: GitHub (organization repository) 
Branching strategy: 
● main: Production-ready; protected — merge only via approved PR. 
● dev: Integration branch — target for all feature PRs. 
● staging: Test in a close to production environment. 
● feat/[purpose]: Feature branch — one GitHub Issue = one branch. 
● fix/[purpose]: Bug fix branch — used to resolve bugs, corresponds to type: fix label. 
● chore/[purpose]: Maintenance branch — update dependencies, config, or tooling; no logic 
changes. 
● docs/[purpose]: Documentation branch — write or update project documentation. 
● refactor/[purpose]: Refactor branch — restructure existing code without changing 
behavior. 
● test/[purpose]: Test branch — write or update test cases. 
PR rules: 
● Minimum 1 reviewer approval required before merge. 
● All CI checks (build, lint, unit tests) must pass before merge is allowed. 
● No direct commits to main or dev.

III. Software Requirement Specification 
1. Product Overview 
The Solar Battery Maintenance System is a platform for monitoring and maintaining lithium-ion 
battery storage installed at solar farms and residential solar sites. Battery packs degrade silently: 
capacity fades, internal resistance rises, and a cell that is about to fail gives very little visible warning 
before it causes an outage or, in the worst case, a thermal event. Today the owner of a site has no 
continuous view of battery condition, technicians are dispatched only after a failure has already 
happened, and the maintenance process itself is tracked in spreadsheets and messages rather than 
in a system that can prove a deadline was met. 
The system closes all three gaps at once. IoT gateways installed next to each battery pack read 
voltage, current, temperature, state of charge and state of health from the battery management 
system and push the measurements to the server every 30 seconds. The readings are stored as a 
TimescaleDB hypertable and streamed live to the web and mobile clients, so an operator sees the 
fleet as it is right now rather than as it was at the last manual inspection. On top of that stream, an AI 
module continuously estimates each pack's state of health and classifies its behaviour as normal, 
degrading or failed. 
When a reading crosses a configured threshold, or when the AI module reports a degraded pack, the 
system raises an alert and — for anything safety-related — opens a maintenance ticket automatically. 
Tickets follow the ITIL service-management model with three priority levels and a hard resolution 
deadline: P1 Critical within 4 hours, P2 High within 24 hours, P3 Standard within 72 hours. A live SLA 
timer runs on every ticket; when it is about to expire the ticket escalates to a higher technician tier 
rather than having its deadline extended. The result is a maintenance process that is auditable end to 
end, from the sensor reading that triggered it to the customer's rating after the technician closed it. 
The platform serves four roles across two clients. Admin, Manager and Staff work on the web 
application; Customer works on the mobile application and is deliberately blocked from the web. 
Staff also has a dedicated mobile experience for field work. Alongside the core monitoring and 
ticketing flows, the system provides a guide and a blog for technical content, an in-ticket chat with 
attachments, translation and AI reply assistance, multi-channel notifications (push, email, SMS), 
energy and carbon analytics derived from the telemetry stream, and full audit logging of every 
privileged action. 
 
37 
 
 
Figure 1.1 – System context diagram 
2. User Requirements 
2.1 Actors 
The platform is used by five human actors and three non-human actors. Route access is decided by 
role alone; the permission codes carried in a session control which actions appear inside a screen 
and never gate a route. 
# Actor Description 
1 Guest 
An unauthenticated visitor. Can browse the public Landing Page on the 
web, register a new Customer account, verify the account by OTP, and 
recover or reactivate an account. Has no access to any operational data. 
2 Customer 
The owner of a site or of individual battery assets. Works on the mobile 
application only — a Customer signing in on the web is redirected to a "use 
the mobile app" notice. Monitors their own assets, receives alerts, raises 
support tickets, chats with the assigned technician, and rates the service 
after resolution. 
3 Staff 
A maintenance technician, graded into three skill tiers (Tier 1 basic, Tier 2 
intermediate, Tier 3 expert). Works on both the web application and a 
field-oriented mobile application. Receives assigned tickets, records 
maintenance logs and device calibrations, and consults the guide on site. 
38 
 
# Actor Description 
4 Manager 
The head of the maintenance team. Works on the web application. Triages 
the ticket queue, assigns tickets to technicians by tier and workload, 
records the impact and urgency from which the ticket priority is derived, 
configures ambient thresholds, and monitors SLA compliance and fleet 
analytics. 
5 Admin 
The system administrator. Works on the web application. Manages 
accounts, roles and the permission matrix, battery types and alert 
thresholds, IoT devices, the SMS gateway, broadcast notifications, and 
reviews all audit logs. 
6 IoT Gateway 
A non-human actor. An edge device ( ESP32 class) installed at a site. 
Authenticates with its own API key, reads the battery management system 
over UART, and pushes sensor readings and a health heartbeat to the 
server. Buffers locally and replays when connectivity is restored. 
7 AI Module 
A non-human actor. A FastAPI service that receives a window of recent 
readings and returns a state-of-health estimate and an anomaly 
classification, together with a confidence score, the model version and the 
measured latency. Its remaining-useful-life output is a research capability 
that no screen consumes, and its prescription endpoint is planned for a 
later sprint and not yet built. 
8 
System 
Scheduler 
A non-human actor. Background workers that evaluate thresholds, escalate 
unacknowledged alerts, expire SLA timers, roll up daily energy summaries, 
dispatch outbox messages and raise preventive-maintenance tickets when 
a schedule falls due. 
Table 2.1 – Actors of the Solar Battery Maintenance System

3. Functional Requirements 
3.1 System Functional Overview 
3.1.1 Screens Flow 
The screen flow is maintained as a single diagram file, docs/Flow_Screen_Diagram_v3.drawio, whose 
six pages match the six flows below one for one: 01 Authentication (Web + Mobile), 02 Admin (Web), 
03 Manager (Web), 04 Staff (Web), 05 Customer (Mobile), 06 Staff (Mobile). Edges that would tangle 
the tree are listed in a CROSS-NAVIGATION note on each page instead of being drawn.

# Notation Name Description 
1 Oval Start / End 
The entry or exit point of a flow, such as application 
start or sign-out. 
2 Diamond Decision 
A branch evaluated by the system, such as "is the 
session valid?" or "is 2FA enabled?". 
3 White rectangle Screen 
A screen reachable by any authenticated role that 
owns the route. 
4 Yellow rectangle Internal screen 
A screen restricted to Admin, Manager or Staff on the 
web application. 
5 Blue rectangle Mobile screen 
A screen that exists only in the mobile application 
(Customer or Staff field use). 
6 
Double-bar 
rectangle 
Screen group 
A set of sibling screens presented together, such as 
the account settings sections or a tab group. 
7 Dashed arrow Cross-navigation 
A navigation edge listed in the CROSS-NAVIGATION 
note of a diagram page instead of being drawn, to 
keep the tree readable. 
Table 3.1 – Screen flow diagram notation 
B.1 Shared Authentication Flow (Web & Mobile) 
● App start — check current session state (hydrating). Web renders a layout skeleton and Mobile 
an activity indicator until hydration finishes, so no navigation decision is taken on an unknown 
session. 
49 
 
● Not logged in (Web) — the public Landing Page is served at / for anonymous visitors; every 
protected route redirects to Login. 
● Not logged in (Mobile) — go to the Login screen. 
● Valid credentials, 2FA disabled — redirect by role. 
● Valid credentials, 2FA enabled — Enter 2FA Code screen, then redirect by role. 
● Sign in with Google — Web completes the exchange on the OAuth callback screen; Mobile uses 
the native Google Sign-In SDK configured once at app boot. 
● Account locked — Reactivate Account screen, then back to Login. 
● No account yet — Register, then Verify OTP, then back to Login (available on both Web and 
Mobile). 
● Invited user — opens the invitation link, sets a password and activates the account (Web). 
● Forgot password — Forgot Password screen, then back to Login. 
● Forgot Password and Reactivate Account are one route each: their multi-step wizards (request 
code, verify code, set new password) are internal reducer state, not separate routes. 
● Confirming a 2FA sign-in from a second device is a standalone screen reachable by any signed-in 
role; on success it lands on Account Settings. 
● Redirect by role: the role router (redirectByRole) sends ADMIN to /admin/dashboard (Web); 
MANAGER to /manager/dashboard (Web); STAFF to /staff/dashboard (Web) or 
(staff)/(tabs)/dashboard (Mobile); CUSTOMER to (customer)/(tabs)/dashboard (Mobile). It 
returns nothing for a role the platform does not serve, which is what produces the two notices 
below. 
● CUSTOMER signing in on Web is blocked and redirected to /use-mobile-app. 
● ADMIN or MANAGER signing in on Mobile is blocked: the tokens that were just issued are 
discarded, the session is cleared, and the user is sent to the "use the Web App" notice. 
● A signed-in user who reaches a screen their role does not own gets 403 Forbidden on Web and is 
redirected back to Login on Mobile. 
● Guard chain: ProtectedRoute then RoleRoute on Web; useAuthGuard plus a per-route-group 
redirect on Mobile. 
● After a successful Mobile sign-in the app registers its device push token as a best-effort 
background step that never blocks the flow. 
B.2 Web App — ADMIN Flow 
● Dashboard is the landing screen. The sidebar has one un-titled top group that is always visible, 
followed by 4 collapsible sections. 
● Top group: Dashboard; Analytics. 
● Infrastructure: Sites to Site Detail; Battery Assets to Battery Asset Detail; Battery Types & 
Thresholds; Battery Alerts; Environmental Incidents; Ambient Config; IoT Devices to 
Add/Detail/Edit Device 
● Support: Tickets to Ticket Detail (with the ticket chat panel); Guide to Create Guide Article / 
Guide Article Detail to Edit Guide Article; Blog to Create Blog Post / Blog Post Detail to Edit Blog 
Post. 
50 
 
● Guide Article Detail can also start a new article seeded from the article currently open, so Create 
Guide Article is reachable both from the list and from an open article. 
● Users: Accounts (account management); Roles & Permissions. 
● System: SMS Gateway; Saga Debug; Audit Logs; Battery Audit Logs; File Access Audit Logs; Send 
Notification (broadcast); Settings. 
● Accounts, Roles & Permissions, Battery Types & Thresholds and SMS Gateway have no child 
routes: create, edit and delete all happen in a dialog on top of the list screen. 
● Account Settings is a single route holding 7 internal sections: personal profile, password & email, 
security, notification devices, notification preferences, sign-in history, and Danger Zone. 
● Profile is a route with no sidebar or menu entry — it is reachable by URL only. The notification 
bell in the top bar opens the unread notification list. 
● Route access is decided by role alone. The permission codes carried in the session control which 
actions are shown inside a screen; they never gate a route. 
B.3 Web App — MANAGER Flow 
● Top group: Dashboard; Analytics. 
● Management group: Sites to Site Detail; Tickets to Ticket Detail; Ticket Queue; Guide to 
Create/Detail/Edit Guide Article; Blog to Create/Detail/Edit Blog Post; Battery Alerts; 
Environmental Incidents; Ambient Config; Calibration Expiring. 
● System group: Settings. 
● Ticket Queue shares the ticket table with the ticket list, so a row click opens Ticket Detail — this 
is how a manager claims and triages a queued ticket. 
● Ticket Detail links into Guide Article Detail through its “related guide articles” panel; Guide 
Article Detail can in turn seed a new article from the one currently open. 
● Ambient Config and Calibration Expiring are independent sidebar entries with no navigation 
between them. 
● Profile is reachable by URL only, as on the Admin side. 
B.4 Web App — STAFF Flow 
● Top group: Dashboard; My Tickets to Ticket Detail; Maintenance History (own records); Guide to 
Create/Detail/Edit Guide Article; Blog to Create/Detail/Edit Blog Post; SLA Monitor; Device 
Calibration (view expiring schedules and record a new calibration); IoT Devices (list + detail). 
● Reports group: Alerts; Battery Alerts; Environmental Incidents. 
● System group: Settings. 
● Staff sees two distinct alert screens: Alerts is a notification and report feed specific to Staff, while 
Battery Alerts is the threshold-alert list shared with Admin and Manager. 
● Maintenance History, SLA Monitor and Alerts all navigate into Ticket Detail; Ticket Detail links 
into Guide Article Detail through its related-articles panel, and Guide Article Detail can seed a 
new article from the one currently open. 
● The three Reports screens are siblings and are never chained one into another. Profile is 
reachable by URL only. 
51 
 
B.5 Mobile App — CUSTOMER Flow 
● The tab bar shows 3 tabs: Dashboard, Tickets, Profile. The Alerts screen belongs to the same tab 
group but is hidden from the bar and is opened from the Dashboard. 
● Dashboard to Alerts (through the bell); to Battery List to Battery Detail; to Blog to Blog Post 
Detail. 
● Battery Detail to Site Detail; to Alert Detail; to Create Ticket. Site Detail leads back into Battery 
Detail. 
● Alerts to Alert Detail to Incident Detail; Alert Detail also links to the related Ticket Detail and 
Battery Detail. 
● Tickets to Create Ticket; to Ticket Detail with chat, attachments, SLA countdown, rating and 
reopen. Create Ticket replaces itself with Ticket Detail once the ticket is filed, and Ticket Detail 
links to the battery the ticket is about. 
● The Guide is not available to CUSTOMER on Mobile; customers read long-form content through 
Blog (Dashboard to Blog to Blog Post Detail). The Guide is a STAFF-only surface on Mobile. 
● Profile to Change Password; 2FA; Login Sessions; Danger Zone (delete account); and to Battery 
Detail for a listed asset. 
● The Settings hub is designed as a flat set of ten sibling entries (Change Email, Change Password, 
Phone Verification, 2FA, Login Sessions, Trusted Devices, Notification Preferences, Notification 
Feed, Permission Catalogue, Danger Zone) plus the Chat Inbox; entries are never chained one 
into another. 
● Edit Profile and Mentions are implemented but currently have no entry point: the profile screen 
edits inline through a modal instead of navigating to Edit Profile, and Mentions is reachable only 
by deep link. 
B.6 Mobile App — STAFF (Field) Flow 
● The tab bar shows 3 tabs: Dashboard, Customers, Profile. The Notifications screen is registered in 
the same tab group but hidden from the bar; it is opened from the Dashboard header. 
● Dashboard is the work queue: to Notifications (through the bell in the header); to Ticket Detail. 
● Notifications to Alerts; to Ticket Detail. 
● Alerts to Alert Detail; and directly to Incident Detail. 
● Customers to Customer Detail, which lists that customer's tickets and opens Ticket Detail. 
● Ticket Detail to Battery Detail; to Guide Article Detail. 
● Battery Detail to Site Detail; to Alert Detail. Site Detail leads back into Battery Detail. 
● Profile to Tools hub; to Maintenance History; to Notification Preferences. These three are 
reached from Profile only, never from each other; Maintenance History opens Ticket Detail. 
● Tools hub to Battery Types to Battery Type Detail; Calibration to Create Calibration; Devices to 
Device Detail and Scan Device; Chat Inbox; Mentions; Permissions catalogue; Blog to Blog Post 
Detail. Chat Inbox and Mentions both open Ticket Detail.

Table 3.4 – Mobile application screen authorization matrix 
The (staff) route group blocks any user whose role is not STAFF and the (customer) route group 
blocks any user whose role is not CUSTOMER — even if authenticated, a role mismatch redirects to 
the Login screen. ADMIN and MANAGER have no screens on Mobile; the only screen they can reach 
is the "use the Web App" notice, which is why rows 5 and 8 of this table are N for every role listed in 
their own table. Rows 21 and 32 are implemented but not yet reachable through navigation: 
Mentions opens only from a deep link, and the profile screen edits inline through a modal instead of 
navigating to Edit Profile. 
3.1.4 Non-Screen Functions 
# Feature group System function Description 
1 Authentication JWT issue and refresh 
Issues a signed access token carrying the account role 
and permission codes, plus a rotating refresh token, 
on every successful sign-in. 
2 Authentication Token storage policy 
Stores tokens in an HTTP-only cookie on the web and 
in Secure Store on mobile; localStorage and 
AsyncStorage are never used. 
3 Authentication Password hashing 
Hashes passwords with BCrypt before persisting them; 
the plaintext never leaves the request scope. 
4 Authentication Failed sign-in lockout 
Counts consecutive failed attempts per account and 
locks the account when the limit is reached, requiring 
reactivation. 
5 Authentication 
Permission 
synchronisation 
Keeps the permission list held in the mobile session 
synchronised with the server while the application is 
running. 
59 
 
# Feature group System function Description 
6 Authentication 
Device push token 
registration 
Registers the device push token after a successful 
mobile sign-in as a best-effort step that never blocks 
the flow. 
7 Telemetry Sensor reading ingestion 
Accepts batched readings from an authenticated 
gateway and appends them to the TimescaleDB 
hypertable. 
8 Telemetry Ingestion idempotency 
Discards a replayed batch by its idempotency key so a 
gateway can safely resend after a connectivity loss. 
9 Telemetry Real-time fan-out 
Pushes new readings to every open dashboard and 
asset detail screen over SignalR, with no manual 
refresh. Subscription is authorised per asset on the 
server, so a client cannot receive another customer's 
telemetry by guessing an identifier. 
10 Telemetry 
Ambient reading 
collection 
Collects site environmental data from IoT sensors, or 
from the Open-Meteo API using the site's GPS 
coordinates where a site has no physical sensor. 
11 Alerts Threshold evaluation 
Evaluates every incoming reading against the active 
threshold version for the asset's battery type and 
raises an alert on breach. 
12 Alerts Deduplication and merge 
Collapses repeats of the same anomaly on the same 
asset into one open alert instead of a new alert per 
reading. 
13 Alerts Site-level aggregation 
Merges five or more assets raising the same anomaly 
at one site inside a time window into a single site 
alert. 
14 Alerts Noise breach damping 
Records short-lived boundary crossings separately so a 
value oscillating around a threshold does not produce 
alert spam. 
15 Alerts 
Acknowledgement 
escalation 
Escalates an alert unacknowledged for 15 minutes to 
Staff and for 30 minutes to Manager, opening a P1 
ticket automatically. 
16 Alerts Silence rule evaluation 
Suppresses alert generation for an asset, a site or a 
battery type while a silence rule is in force. 
17 AI State-of-health inference 
Sends a window of recent readings to the AI module 
and caches the returned health estimate with its 
confidence, model version and latency. 
18 AI Anomaly classification 
Caches the normal, degrading or failed classification 
returned by the anomaly model and retains technician 
feedback against it. 
19 AI Automatic ticket creation 
Opens a ticket at the priority implied by the anomaly 
severity when the AI module reports a degraded or 
failed pack. 
20 Tickets SLA timer 
Starts a countdown at intake against the priority 
deadline of 4, 24 or 72 hours and records pause 
events. 
60 
 
# Feature group System function Description 
21 Tickets SLA breach escalation 
Escalates a breaching ticket to a higher technician tier 
without extending the original deadline. 
22 Tickets Activity timeline 
Appends an immutable entry for every state change, 
assignment and comment on a ticket. 
23 Tickets Unread message count 
Maintains a per-user unread count per ticket so a 
thread awaiting a reply is visible in the list. 
24 Tickets 
Preventive maintenance 
trigger 
Raises a ticket automatically when a maintenance 
schedule falls due for an asset. 
25 Chat Read-state tracking 
Marks a message read for a participant when it is 
displayed and propagates the receipt to the other 
participants. 
26 Chat Message translation 
Translates a message into a target language on 
demand and caches the result per user. 
27 Notifications Multi-channel dispatch 
Routes each notification to push, email and SMS 
according to the recipient's preferences and the 
enabled channels. 
28 Notifications Template rendering 
Renders notification content from a stored template 
so wording stays consistent across channels. 
29 Energy Session detection 
Groups the reading stream into continuous charge and 
discharge sessions with their energy, start and end 
state of charge and peak power. 
30 Energy Cycle logging 
Closes a complete charge-discharge cycle and 
computes depth of discharge, round-trip efficiency 
and a stress score fed back to the health model. 
31 Energy Daily roll-up 
Aggregates sessions into per-asset and per-site daily 
energy, cost saving and avoided CO2 figures using the 
regional tariff and emission factor. 
32 IoT Heartbeat monitoring 
Marks a gateway offline when its 60-second heartbeat 
stops and raises a device alert. 
33 IoT Firmware rollout tracking 
Records the from and to version and the outcome of 
every over-the-air update per device. 
34 IoT Calibration expiry watch 
Flags sensor calibrations that are approaching expiry 
so accuracy does not silently drift. 
35 Platform Outbox dispatch 
Publishes domain events to the message broker 
through a transactional outbox so a database commit 
and an event are never out of step. 
36 Platform Saga orchestration 
Coordinates workflows that span services and 
compensates the completed steps when a later step 
fails. 
37 Platform Audit aggregation 
Collects audit records emitted by every service into a 
single queryable trail. 
38 Platform File access audit 
Records every upload and download with the actor, 
the file purpose and the outcome. 
61 
 
# Feature group System function Description 
39 Platform Malware scanning 
Scans every uploaded object before it becomes 
attachable; a file that fails the scan is deleted from 
storage and never reaches a record. 
40 Platform Chat content safety 
Detects personally identifiable information, filters 
profanity and scores spam on a chat message before it 
is stored. 
41 Platform Translation term masking 
Masks technical terms and identifiers before a 
message is translated and restores them afterwards, 
so a translated message stays technically correct. 
42 Platform Route authorisation 
Decides route access by role alone; permission codes 
carried in the session control which actions are 
offered inside a screen and never gate a route. 
Table 3.5 – Non-screen system functions 
3.1.5 Entity Relationship Diagram 
The platform is split into nine services, seven of which own a database schema: Auth, Battery, Ticket, 
Notification, FileStorage, Sms and AuditAggregator. The remaining two hold no persistent data of 
their own — ApiGateway is a reverse proxy that only routes requests, and EmailService keeps 
transient delivery state in Redis rather than in a relational database. Each of the seven schemas lives 
in its own database, provisioned separately at start-up. BatteryAsset and Site are the centre of 
gravity: telemetry, alerts, AI results, energy analytics and IoT device records all resolve back to them. 
Cross-service links are logical references by identifier, not foreign keys, so a service can be deployed 
and migrated independently. 
 
Figure 3.2 – Entity relationship diagram — conceptual 
Entities description

# Entity Service Description 
1 Account Auth 
A user account with credentials, status and a single 
assigned role. 
2 AccountProfile Auth 
Personal profile attached to an account: avatar, address, 
birth date and time zone. 
3 StaffProfile Auth 
Technician profile carrying the employee code, 
department, skill tier and concurrent-ticket capacity. 
4 StaffSkill Auth 
A skill held by a technician, with level and certification 
expiry, used for assignment matching. 
5 Role Auth 
A named role: Admin, Manager, Staff or Customer. One 
role serves many accounts. 
6 Permission Auth 
An atomic permission code in the form module.action, for 
example battery.view or ticket.assign. 
7 RolePermission Auth The permission matrix linking roles to permission codes. 
8 RefreshToken Auth 
A rotating refresh token; the replaced-by chain allows 
replay detection. 
9 TrustedDevice Auth 
A device the user marked as trusted so that the two-factor 
challenge is skipped until it expires. 
10 BackupCode Auth 
A single-use recovery code for two-factor authentication; 
at most eight per account. 
11 LoginAttempt Auth 
Append-only history of sign-in attempts shown to the 
user, with result, method and device. 
12 AccountMergeLog Auth 
A record of an administrator merging two accounts, 
keeping the conflict resolution and a snapshot of the 
secondary account. 
13 AuditLog Auth 
Forensic log of sensitive actions: sign-in, password change, 
account lock, role change and forced logout. 
14 AuditOutbox Auth 
Outbox dedicated to the audit pipeline, publishing audit 
events to the aggregator. 
15 Site Battery 
A physical location that groups battery assets and ambient 
sensors.

An individual battery pack installed at a site, with its state

Table 3.6 – Entity descriptions 
3.2 Authentication & Account 
3.2.1 Account Registration 
Function trigger: Mobile app → Login screen → tap "Register"; or Web → Login page → click "Don't 
have an account?  Sign up". 
Function description:  
● Actor: Guest 
● Purpose: Create a Customer account so the visitor can access their battery assets. 
● Data processing: Validates the submitted fields, checks that the email is not already bound to an 
active account, hashes the password with a salted adaptive algorithm, persists the account with 
status Inactive, generates a one-time code and hands it to EmailService through the transactional 
outbox. 
Function detail:  
● Data & Validation: Email must be a valid address and not already registered. Password must be 
at least 8 characters and contain an uppercase letter, a lowercase letter, a digit and a special 
character. The confirmation field must match. Phone number, when supplied, must be a valid 
Vietnamese mobile number. 
● Normal case: The visitor submits valid details → the system creates the account with status 
Inactive → an email carrying the one-time code is queued → the app navigates to Verify OTP → 
the visitor enters the code within its validity window → the account becomes Active → the app 
returns to Login. 
● Abnormal case: 
○ The email is already bound to an active account → MSG-ERR-04 is shown and no account is 
created. 
○ The password does not satisfy the policy → MSG-ERR-06 is shown and the form is not 
submitted. 
○ The code is wrong or has expired → MSG-ERR-05 is shown; the visitor may request a new 
code. 
○ Email delivery fails → the account stays Inactive and the visitor can request the code again 
from the Verify OTP screen. 
● Business rules: BR-01, BR-02, BR-04 (see section 5.1). 
● Screen layout: Register (Web), Register (Mobile), Verify OTP 
68 
 
 
Figure 3.3 – Register (Web) 
 
Figure 3.4 – Register (Mobile) 
69 
 
 
Figure 3.5 – Verify OTP 
3.2.2 Standard Sign-in 
Function trigger: Web → Login page → click "Log in"; Mobile → Login screen → tap "Login". 
Function description:  
● Actor: Guest, Customer, Staff, Manager, Admin 
● Purpose: Authenticate the user and establish a session carrying their role and permissions. The 
Login screen offers two methods side by side: email and password, or an existing Google 
account. 
● Data processing: Compares the submitted password against the stored hash, records the 
attempt, and on success issues a signed access token containing the account role and permission 
codes plus a rotating refresh token. The web stores the tokens in an HTTP-only cookie, the 
mobile app in the Secure Store. The role router then decides the landing screen. 
Function detail:  
● Data & Validation: Email and password are both required and must be well formed. 
● Normal case: Correct credentials and 2FA disabled → tokens are issued → the role router sends 
Admin, Manager and Staff to their web dashboard and Customer and Staff to their mobile 
dashboard. With 2FA enabled the flow stops at the Enter 2FA Code screen first. 
● Abnormal case: 
○ The credentials do not match → MSG-ERR-01 is shown and the failed-attempt counter 
increases. 
○ The counter reaches its limit → the account is locked and MSG-ERR-02 directs the user to the 
reactivation flow. 
○ The account has never been verified → MSG-ERR-03 is shown. 
70 
 
○ A Customer signs in on the web → the session is discarded and the browser is redirected to 
the "use the mobile app" notice (MSG-ERR-09). 
○ An Admin or Manager signs in on mobile → the tokens that were just issued are discarded, 
the session is cleared and the "use the web app" notice is shown (MSG-ERR-10). 
● Business rules: BR-05, BR-06, BR-07 (see section 5.1). 
● Screen layout: Login (Web), Login (Mobile), Use Mobile App notice, Use Web App notice 
 
Figure 3.6 – Login (Web) 
 
71 
 
Figure 3.7 – Login (Mobile) 
 
Figure 3.8 – Use Mobile App notice 
 
Figure 3.9 – Use Web App notice 
3.2.3 Sign-in with Google 
Function trigger: Login screen → "Log in with Google". 
Function description:  
72 
 
● Actor: Guest, Customer, Staff, Manager, Admin 
● Purpose: Let the user authenticate with an existing Google identity instead of a platform 
password. This is the second of the two sign-in methods offered on the Login screen. 
● Data processing: The web redirects to Google and completes the code exchange on the OAuth 
callback route; the mobile app uses the native Google Sign-In SDK configured once at application 
boot. The returned identity token is verified, matched against an existing account by email, and 
either linked to that account or used to provision a new Customer account. Platform tokens are 
then issued exactly as for a standard sign-in. 
Function detail:  
● Data & Validation: The identity token must be valid, unexpired and issued for this application's 
client id. 
● Normal case: Google returns a verified identity → an account with that email exists → the 
identities are linked, the merge is recorded, and tokens are issued → the role router decides the 
landing screen. If no account exists, a Customer account is provisioned in Active state and the 
same path continues. 
● Abnormal case: 
○ The user cancels the Google consent screen → the app returns to Login with no change. 
○ The identity token fails verification → MSG-ERR-20 is shown and no session is created. 
○ The email belongs to a locked account → MSG-ERR-02 is shown and the reactivation flow is 
offered. 
● Business rules: BR-01, BR-06 (see section 5.1). 
● Screen layout: Login (Web), Login (Mobile) 
 
Figure 3.10 – Login (Web) 
73 
 
 
Figure 3.11 – Login (Mobile) 
3.2.4 Two-Factor Authentication 
Function trigger: Automatically after a successful password check when 2FA is enabled on the 
account. 
Function description:  
● Actor: Customer, Staff, Manager, Admin 
● Purpose: Add a second proof of identity before the session is granted. 
● Data processing: The second factor is a time-based one-time password from an authenticator 
app, with an SMS code and single-use backup codes as alternatives. The enrolled secret is 
encrypted at rest. The user may instead approve the attempt from a device already marked 
trusted, which receives the prompt over SignalR. Tokens are released only after one of these 
succeeds. 
Function detail:  
● Data & Validation: The code must be six digits and used inside the tolerance window of the 
time-based algorithm. A backup code may be used once only and is consumed on use. 
● Normal case: The user enters a valid code → the session is released and the role router decides 
the landing screen. Alternatively the user approves the attempt on a trusted device through the 
cross-device confirmation screen, which lands on Account Settings on success. 
● Abnormal case: 
○ The code is wrong or expired → MSG-ERR-05 is shown and the user may request another. 
○ The user has lost the second factor → a backup code is accepted and is then consumed. 
○ The approval is not given within the window → the sign-in attempt is abandoned and the 
user returns to Login. 
74 
 
● Business rules: BR-05 (see section 5.1). 
● Screen layout: Two-Factor Authentication setup 
 
Figure 3.12 – Two-Factor Authentication setup 
3.2.5 Password Recovery 
Function trigger: Login screen → "Forget password". 
Function description:  
● Actor: Guest 
● Purpose: Let a user who cannot sign in restore access without contacting an administrator. 
● Data processing: Accepts an email address, and — only when it belongs to an account — issues a 
time-limited reset code by email. Verifies the code, then replaces the stored hash with the hash 
of the new password and revokes every existing refresh token for that account. 
Function detail:  
● Data & Validation: The email must be well formed. The new password must satisfy the password 
policy and match its confirmation. 
● Normal case: The user submits their email → the same generic confirmation is returned 
regardless of whether the account exists → the user enters the emailed code → sets a new 
password → every existing session is revoked → the user returns to Login. The three steps live 
on one route and keep their position in the component state. 
● Abnormal case: 
○ The code is wrong or expired → MSG-ERR-05 is shown and the user may restart the request. 
○ The new password does not satisfy the policy → MSG-ERR-06 is shown. 
○ The email does not belong to any account → the same generic confirmation is still returned, 
so the endpoint cannot be used to discover which emails are registered. 
75 
 
● Business rules: BR-03, BR-05 (see section 5.1). 
● Screen layout: Forgot Password 
 
Figure 3.13 – Forgot Password 
3.2.6 Account Reactivation 
Function trigger: Login screen → "Restore account", or automatically offered after MSG-ERR-02. 
Function description:  
● Actor: Guest 
● Purpose: Restore an account locked by repeated failed sign-in attempts. 
● Data processing: Issues a verification code to the registered email, verifies it, clears the 
failed-attempt counter, restores the account to Active and asks the user to set a new password. 
Function detail:  
● Data & Validation: The verification code must be entered within its validity window; the new 
password must satisfy the password policy. 
● Normal case: The user requests reactivation → enters the emailed code → sets a new password 
→ the account returns to Active and the failed-attempt counter is reset → the user returns to 
Login. 
● Abnormal case: 
○ The code is wrong or expired → MSG-ERR-05 is shown. 
○ The account is not in a locked state → the flow reports that no action is required and returns 
to Login. 
● Business rules: BR-05 (see section 5.1). 
● Screen layout: Reactivate Account 
76 
 
 
Figure 3.14 – Reactivate Account 
3.2.7 Accept Invitation 
Function trigger: The invited user opens the invitation link received by email (web only). 
Function description:  
● Actor: Guest 
● Purpose: Let an invited internal user activate the account created for them by Admin. 
● Data processing: Validates the single-use invitation token, shows the pre-filled email, accepts a 
password, activates the account with the role chosen by Admin at invitation time and consumes 
the token. 
Function detail:  
● Data & Validation: The invitation token must be valid, unexpired and unused. The password 
must satisfy the password policy. 
● Normal case: The invited user opens a valid link → sets a password → the account becomes 
Active with the assigned role → the user is sent to Login. 
● Abnormal case: 
○ The token has expired or has already been used → an explanatory message is shown and the 
user is asked to request a new invitation. 
○ The password does not satisfy the policy → MSG-ERR-06 is shown. 
● Business rules: BR-04 (see section 5.1). 
● Screen layout: Accept Invitation 
77 
 
 
Figure 3.15 – Accept Invitation 
3.2.8 Manage Own Profile and Credentials 
Function trigger: Web → Account Settings; Mobile → Profile tab → Settings hub. 
Function description:  
● Actor: Customer, Staff, Manager, Admin 
● Purpose: Let a user keep their own identity data, credentials and preferences current. 
● Data processing: Account Settings is a single route holding seven sections — personal profile, 
password and email, security, notification devices, notification preferences, sign-in history and 
Danger Zone — with each section's step kept in component state. Changing the email requires 
verification of the new address; changing the password requires the current password and 
revokes other sessions. 
Function detail:  
● Data & Validation: Email changes must target an address not already registered. Password 
changes require the current password and a new one satisfying the policy. Phone verification 
requires a code delivered by SMS. 
● Normal case: The user edits a section and saves → the change is persisted and confirmed → for 
email and password changes the affected sessions are revoked and the user re-authenticates. 
● Abnormal case: 
○ The new email is already registered → MSG-ERR-04 is shown. 
○ The current password is wrong → MSG-ERR-01 is shown and nothing is changed. 
○ The SMS gateway is disabled → phone verification is unavailable and the section explains 
why. 
● Business rules: BR-01, BR-07 (see section 5.1). 
● Screen layout: Account Settings (Web), Settings hub and sub-screens (Mobile) 
78 
 
 
Figure 3.16 – Account Settings (Web) 
 
Figure 3.17 – Settings hub and sub-screens (Mobile) 
3.2.9 Manage Sessions and Trusted Devices 
Function trigger: Account Settings → Security; Mobile → Settings hub → Login Sessions / Trusted 
Devices. 
Function description:  
79 
 
● Actor: Customer, Staff, Manager, Admin 
● Purpose: Give the user visibility and control over where their account is signed in. 
● Data processing: Lists active sessions with device, location and last activity derived from the 
sign-in attempt records, and lists devices marked trusted for two-factor authentication. Revoking 
a session invalidates its refresh token immediately. 
Function detail:  
● Data & Validation: A user may only see and revoke their own sessions and devices. 
● Normal case: The user opens the list → selects a session or a device → confirms revocation → 
the entry disappears and any client holding that token is signed out at its next request. 
● Abnormal case: 
○ The user revokes the session they are currently using → they are signed out and returned to 
Login. 
○ The session has already expired → it is removed from the list without an error. 
● Business rules: BR-07, BR-08 (see section 5.1). 
● Screen layout: Account Settings — Security 
 
Figure 3.18 – Account Settings — Security 
3.2.10 Delete Own Account 
Function trigger: Mobile → Settings hub → Danger Zone. 
Function description:  
● Actor: Customer 
● Purpose: Let a customer permanently give up access to the platform. 
● Data processing: Requires an explicit typed confirmation, then deactivates the account, revokes 
every refresh token and unregisters every push device token. Operational records the account 
80 
 
produced — tickets, alerts, maintenance history — are retained for audit and are detached from 
the identity rather than deleted. 
Function detail:  
● Data & Validation: The confirmation phrase must be typed exactly; the account password is 
requested again. 
● Normal case: The customer confirms → the account is deactivated, all sessions and device 
tokens are revoked → the app returns to Login. 
● Abnormal case: 
○ The confirmation phrase does not match → the action stays disabled. 
○ The customer still has an open ticket → the action explains that the open ticket will be 
closed and asks for confirmation again. 
● Business rules: BR-36 (see section 5.1). 
● Screen layout: Danger Zone 
 
Figure 3.19 – Danger Zone 
3.2.11 Manage Accounts and Invitations 
Function trigger: Web → Users → Accounts. 
Function description:  
● Actor: Admin 
● Purpose: Give Admin full control over who may use the platform and in which role. 
● Data processing: Create, edit, lock and unlock are performed in a dialog over the account list 
rather than on a child route. Creating an internal account issues a single-use invitation link by 
email instead of setting a password directly. Assigning a role rewrites the permission codes 
carried in that account's next session. 
81 
 
Function detail:  
● Data & Validation: Email must be unique. A role must be selected. Locking requires a reason that 
is written to the audit trail. 
● Normal case: Admin opens Accounts → creates or edits an account in the dialog → saves → the 
change is audit-logged and the list refreshes. For a new internal account an invitation email is 
queued. 
● Abnormal case: 
○ The email is already registered → MSG-ERR-04 is shown. 
○ Admin attempts to lock their own account → the action is refused to avoid locking the last 
administrator out. 
○ The invitation email cannot be delivered → the account stays pending and the invitation can 
be resent from the list. 
● Business rules: BR-01, BR-04, BR-36 (see section 5.1). 
● Screen layout: Accounts 
 
Figure 3.20 – Accounts 
3.2.12 Manage Roles and Permissions 
Function trigger: Web → Users → Roles & Permissions. 
Function description:  
● Actor: Admin 
● Purpose: Define what each role is allowed to do inside a screen. 
● Data processing: Presents the permission matrix of role against permission code. Saving rewrites 
the role's permission set; sessions already issued pick up the change at their next permission 
synchronisation. Route access is not affected, because routes are gated by role alone. 
82 
 
Function detail:  
● Data & Validation: A role must keep at least the permissions required to reach its own 
dashboard. 
● Normal case: Admin toggles permissions in the matrix → saves → the change is audit-logged → 
clients holding an affected session refresh their permission list and the corresponding actions 
appear or disappear inside screens. 
● Abnormal case: 
○ Admin removes a permission the platform requires for that role to function → the change is 
refused with an explanation. 
○ Two administrators edit the same role concurrently → the second save is rejected and the 
newer state is shown for review. 
● Business rules: BR-07, BR-36 (see section 5.1). 
● Screen layout: Roles & Permissions
