Figure 3.103 – Maintenance History 
3.13 Third-party Data Import 
A solar operator that already runs a fleet does not start from an empty system. The customers, the 
sites and the battery packs exist on paper — usually in a spreadsheet handed over by the party that 
installed them. This section covers bringing that handover file into the platform in one controlled 
operation instead of retyping it. 
3.13.1 Import Third-party Data 
Function trigger: Web → Admin → Third-party import. 
Function description: 
Actor: Admin 
Purpose: Load a fleet handed over by an installer or a partner without retyping it, and without 
risking the live database if the file turns out to be wrong. 
Data processing: Three kinds of record can be imported, and they depend on each other in that 
order: a customer, a site belonging to that customer, and a battery asset standing at that site. Each 
row carries the external code the partner uses, so a site can name its customer and an asset can 
name its site by code rather than by an identifier the partner does not have. IoT gateways are 
deliberately excluded: the system issues each gateway together with its API key and MQTT 
credentials, so a third party cannot bring one into existence from a file. The work is split into two 
passes. The first pass parses and validates and writes no business record at all; it only stores the 
batch, its rows and their verdicts. The second pass, started explicitly by the Admin, writes the valid 
rows and is carried out by a background worker, so a large file does not hold the screen. Customer 
accounts are not created locally — the batch asks AuthService for them over the message bus and 
waits, which is why waiting for accounts is reported as its own state rather than being folded into 
writing. 
146 
 
Function detail: 
Data & Validation: An upload may be at most 10 MB and must be CSV with the header row of the 
template. Every required column must be present and every referenced code must resolve — either 
to a row inside the same batch or to something imported earlier. A file whose exact content has 
already been uploaded is refused rather than duplicated. Only an Admin may use any part of this 
function: it creates customer accounts in another service and can remove records in bulk when 
reverted, which is administration of the system rather than day-to-day operation, so it is not opened 
to the Manager. 
Normal case: The Admin downloads the template for each kind of record → fills it in → uploads the 
files and runs the dry-run → the screen reports how many rows are valid and lists every row that is 
not, with the reason → the Admin fixes the source file and repeats, or accepts the result and presses 
Commit → the background worker writes the valid rows and the progress is shown until the batch 
completes. 
Abnormal case: 
The file is malformed or a required column is missing → the batch is refused as unusable and 
MSG-ERR-21 is shown; nothing is stored. 
The same file content was uploaded before → the upload is refused with MSG-ERR-22 rather than 
creating the same records twice. 
A row names a customer or site code that exists nowhere → that row is marked invalid with the 
reason, the remaining rows stay valid, and the batch can still be committed for them. 
Every row in the file is invalid → the batch can never be committed and stays waiting until it is 
abandoned. 
The import turns out to be wrong after committing → the Admin reverts it and the sites, battery 
assets and devices it created are removed. Customer accounts are kept on purpose: they may already 
have been used to sign in or be attached to a ticket, so deleting them would destroy history that no 
longer belongs to the batch. 
Business rules: BR-41, BR-42 (see section 5.1). 
Screen layout: Third-party Import 
147 
 
 
Figure 3.104 – Third-party Import 
 
Figure 3.105 – Third-party Import — validation result 
3.14 System Administration 
The screens in this section keep the platform itself running rather than serving a customer request: 
the hardware that actually sends the SMS messages, the record of who did what, and the wording 
and audience of everything the system sends out. All of them are restricted to the Admin. 
148 
 
3.14.1 Manage the SMS Gateway 
Function trigger: Web → Admin → System → SMS Gateway. 
Function description: 
Actor: Admin 
Purpose: Register the Android handsets that physically send the platform's SMS messages, and keep 
each one inside a daily quota. 
Data processing: A gateway device is registered with a name, a code and a daily limit, and receives 
an API key that it uses to pull pending messages and report the result. The key is shown in plaintext 
once, at creation, and only its hash is stored afterwards. The list shows how many messages each 
device has sent today against its limit, when it was last active, and whether it is currently reachable. 
Function detail: 
Data & Validation: The device code must be unique. Only an Admin may register, revoke or inspect a 
device. A revoked device keeps its history but can no longer authenticate. 
Normal case: The Admin registers a device → copies the API key once and stores it on the handset 
→ the handset starts polling for pending messages → the list shows it as active with its daily count. 
Abnormal case: 
The device code is already taken → the registration is refused. 
The Admin loses the API key → it cannot be shown again; the device must be revoked and registered 
anew. 
A device reaches its daily limit → it stops receiving messages until the next day. 
Business rules: BR-45 (see section 5.1). 
Screen layout: SMS Gateway 
 
Figure 3.106 – SMS Gateway 
149 
 
3.14.2 Review Audit Logs 
Function trigger: Web → Admin → System → Logs. 
Function description: 
Actor: Admin 
Purpose: Answer who did what, when, and from where — after the fact and without relying on 
anyone's memory. 
Data processing: Every service writes what it did to its own audit trail and an aggregator collects 
them into one searchable history. Each entry records the time, the action, whether it succeeded, the 
account involved and the originating address; opening an entry shows the full detail behind it. 
Function detail: 
Data & Validation: The log is read-only — no one, including the Admin, may edit or delete an entry, 
because a record that can be altered is not evidence. Only an Admin may read it. 
Normal case: The Admin searches by action, account or address → the matching entries are listed 
newest first → opening one shows the full detail. 
Abnormal case: 
A search matches nothing → an empty result is shown rather than an error. 
A service is temporarily unable to forward its entries → they are relayed later from its outbox instead 
of being lost. 
Business rules: BR-46 (see section 5.1). 
Screen layout: Audit Logs 
 
Figure 3.107 – Audit Logs 
150 
 
3.14.3 Manage Notification Templates and Audiences 
Function trigger: Web → Admin → Notifications → Notification templates / Notification groups / 
Send history. 
Function description: 
Actor: Admin 
Purpose: Control the wording of everything the system sends, who receives a broadcast, and what 
was actually sent. 
Data processing: Every notification takes its content from a template chosen by the pair of 
notification type and channel, and exactly one version of each pair is in use at a time; editing a 
template creates a new version rather than overwriting the old one, so a change can be rolled back. 
A template may be previewed or test-sent before being activated. Recipients are addressed through 
groups: the system groups follow a role and update their membership automatically as accounts are 
created or deactivated. Every broadcast is recorded as one send, whatever the number of recipients, 
together with how many were delivered and how many were read. 
Function detail: 
Data & Validation: A template may only reference variables that exist for its notification type. A send 
needs at least one recipient group or user and at least one channel. Only an Admin may edit 
templates, manage groups or broadcast. 
Normal case: The Admin edits a template → previews it → activates the new version → composes a 
broadcast against a group → the send is recorded and its delivery and read counts can be inspected 
afterwards. 
Abnormal case: 
A template references a variable that does not exist for its type → it cannot be activated and the 
problem is reported. 
A group resolves to no active recipient → the send is refused rather than recorded as an empty send. 
A recipient has switched the channel off in their preferences → they are skipped and the send 
reports how many were skipped. 
Business rules: BR-47 (see section 5.1). 
Screen layout: Notification Templates, Notification Groups, Send History 
151 
 
 
Figure 3.108 – Notification Templates 
 
Figure 3.109 – Notification Groups 
152 
 
 
Figure 3.110 – Send History 
4. Non-Functional Requirements 
4.1 External Interfaces 
● User Interfaces: The system provides a responsive web application for Admin, Manager and Staff 
and a React Native mobile application for Customer and for Staff field work. Layout adapts from 
desktop to phone; charts and data tables stay legible and interactive with both pointer and touch 
input. A Customer reaching the web application is redirected to a notice directing them to the 
mobile app, and an Admin or Manager reaching the mobile application is signed out and shown 
the equivalent web notice. 
● Hardware Interfaces: IoT gateways read the battery management system over RS485/Modbus, 
CAN bus or UART and publish over MQTT across Wi-Fi or 4G, which the platform bridges into the 
ingestion path. A gateway buffers readings locally while connectivity is lost and replays them 
when it returns; the server discards replayed batches idempotently. A software simulator 
reproduces the same protocol so the full flow can be demonstrated without physical hardware. 
● Software Interfaces: The AI module is consumed over HTTP as a FastAPI service returning a 
state-of-health estimate and an anomaly classification with a confidence score, a model version 
and the measured latency. Its remaining-useful-life output is a research capability that no screen 
consumes, and its prescription endpoint is planned for a later sprint and not yet built. Weather 
data is pulled from Open-Meteo using the site GPS coordinates. Google OAuth 2.0 provides 
federated sign-in. Object storage is S3-compatible (MinIO). Services communicate 
asynchronously through RabbitMQ using a transactional outbox. 
● Communication Interfaces: All client to server traffic uses HTTPS, and real-time streams use 
SignalR over secure WebSockets. Gateways authenticate with a per-device API key that can be 
153 
 
rotated without redeploying the device. Notification delivery uses platform push services, SMTP 
for email and the registered SMS gateway for text messages. 
4.2 Quality Attributes 
4.2.1 Usability 
● Learnability: A new technician should be able to find their assigned work, open a ticket and 
record a maintenance log without training beyond a short walkthrough, because the field 
workflow follows the same order on web and mobile. 
● Consistency: Navigation structure is consistent per platform: the web uses a sidebar grouped by 
domain, and the mobile app uses a tab bar of three to four tabs with detail screens pushed on 
top. 
● Feedback: Every action that changes state produces an explicit confirmation or error message 
drawn from the application message list in section 5.3, in the user's language. 
● Accessibility: Colour is never the only carrier of meaning: alert severity and ticket priority are 
always accompanied by a label or an icon. 
4.2.2 Reliability 
● Availability: The monitoring and alerting path is the availability-critical path; it must keep 
accepting and evaluating readings even while non-core features are degraded. 
● Fault tolerance: If the AI module is unavailable, threshold-based alerting and the whole ticket 
workflow continue to operate; health estimates stop refreshing and the last cached value is 
shown with its timestamp. No safety-path behaviour waits on an inference. 
● Data integrity: A database commit and the domain event it implies are never out of step, 
because events are published through a transactional outbox rather than inline. 
● Recoverability: A gateway that loses connectivity buffers locally and replays on reconnect; 
replayed batches are discarded by idempotency key, so no reading is lost and none is counted 
twice. 
4.2.3 Performance 
● Ingestion latency: A reading pushed by a gateway should be persisted and evaluated against 
thresholds quickly enough that a safety-relevant breach reaches the operator within seconds 
rather than minutes. 
● AI inference latency: A single state-of-health inference should complete in under 100 ms so that 
a prediction can be attached to an alert without delaying it. 
● Prediction accuracy: State-of-health estimation targets a mean absolute error below 2 percent 
on the validation split of the reference dataset. 
● Query performance: Historical telemetry queries stay responsive as the archive grows, because 
readings are stored in a TimescaleDB hypertable and analytics read from pre-computed daily 
roll-ups rather than from raw readings. 
● Real-time fan-out: Open dashboards receive new readings, chat messages and notifications by 
push, without polling and without a manual refresh. 
154 
 
4.2.4 Security 
● Credential protection: Passwords are stored only as salted adaptive hashes. Tokens live in an 
HTTP-only cookie on the web and in Secure Store on mobile; localStorage and AsyncStorage are 
never used for credentials. 
● Access control: Route access is decided by role alone and is enforced on the server as well as in 
the client. Permission codes carried in the session control which actions are offered inside a 
screen and never gate a route. 
● Platform separation: Customer accounts cannot use the web application and Admin or Manager 
accounts cannot use the mobile application; in the latter case the tokens that were just issued 
are discarded and the session is cleared. 
● Device authentication: Each IoT gateway holds its own API key, which can be rotated 
independently so a compromised device can be cut off without touching the rest of the fleet. 
● Auditability: Every privileged action and every file access is recorded with actor, timestamp and 
outcome, and aggregated into a single queryable trail. 
4.2.5 Maintainability 
● Architecture: The backend is split into nine services, each organised in Clean Architecture layers 
(Api, Application, Domain, Infrastructure) so a domain rule can be changed without touching 
transport or persistence code. 
● Shared contracts: Cross-service message and DTO definitions live in a shared contracts library, so 
a producer and a consumer cannot drift apart silently. 
● Feature-sliced clients: The web application is organised by role and feature rather than by 
technical layer, so the screens belonging to one role are co-located. 
● Observability: Structured logs, saga state inspection and audit aggregation make it possible to 
reconstruct what happened during a failed cross-service workflow. 
● Automation: Lint, test and security scanning run in CI on every change, and deployment is 
containerised so an environment can be reproduced. 
4.2.6 Timeliness 
● Alert delivery: A safety-relevant alert is pushed to the customer's device and to the internal 
operators as soon as it is raised, not on the next poll. 
● SLA enforcement: The countdown on a ticket is evaluated continuously; a ticket that is about to 
breach escalates to a higher technician tier rather than having its deadline extended. 
● Escalation ladder: An unacknowledged alert escalates on a fixed ladder — 15 minutes to Staff, 30 
minutes to Manager with an automatic P1 ticket — so no alert can sit unowned. 
5. Requirement Appendix 
5.1 Business Rules 
ID Rule definition 
BR-01 An email address may be bound to at most one active account. 
BR-02 A newly registered Customer account stays inactive until the emailed one-time code is verified. 
155 
 
ID Rule definition 
BR-03 
Password recovery always returns the same generic response whether or not the email exists, 
so the endpoint cannot be used to enumerate accounts. 
BR-04 
Only Admin may create internal accounts (Staff, Manager, Admin); Customer accounts are 
created by self-registration or by Admin invitation. 
BR-05 
An account is locked after a configured number of consecutive failed sign-in attempts and can 
only be restored through the reactivation flow. 
BR-06 
A Customer account may not sign in to the web application; a Manager or Admin account may 
not sign in to the mobile application, and any tokens already issued in that attempt are 
discarded. 
BR-07 
Route access is decided by role alone. Permission codes control which actions are shown inside 
a screen and never gate a route. 
BR-08 
A Customer sees only the sites, assets, alerts and tickets they own; Staff sees only tickets 
assigned to them; Manager and Admin see all. 
BR-09 
A battery asset is identified by its serial number, which is unique across the platform and cannot 
be changed after registration. 
BR-10 A battery asset must belong to exactly one site and exactly one battery type. 
BR-11 
A battery type may have many threshold versions but exactly one active version at a time; 
changing thresholds creates a new version rather than editing the old one. 
BR-12 A state of health below 80 percent marks the pack as due for replacement. 
BR-13 
Sensor readings are append-only. A reading is never updated or deleted; a correction is 
expressed as a calibration record. 
BR-14 
An ingestion batch carrying an idempotency key already seen is discarded without being stored 
twice. 
BR-15 
Repeats of the same anomaly on the same asset are merged into the single open alert instead 
of creating a new alert per reading. 
BR-16 
When five or more assets at one site raise the same anomaly inside the aggregation window, 
the individual alerts are merged into a single site alert. 
BR-17 
An alert not acknowledged within 15 minutes escalates to Staff; at 30 minutes it escalates to 
Manager and a P1 ticket is opened automatically. 
BR-18 
While a silence rule covering an asset, a site or a battery type is in force, matching alerts are 
suppressed and recorded as silenced rather than delivered. 
BR-19 
An environmental incident is more severe than a threshold alert and may spawn one; it can 
never be closed automatically. 
BR-20 
Ticket priority is derived from the impact scope and the urgency the Manager selects at intake, 
using the ITIL impact-versus-urgency matrix. It is written once and does not change afterwards. 
BR-21 
The resolution deadline follows the priority: P1 Critical 4 hours, P2 High 24 hours, P3 Standard 
72 hours. 
BR-22 
A ticket that breaches its SLA escalates to a higher technician tier; the original deadline is never 
extended. 
BR-23 
A ticket moves Open → Pending → InProgress → Completed → Closed. Request and ReAssign 
handle an escalation, and ClosedRejected records a ticket the manager refused while it was still 
Open. 
BR-24 Only the Manager may reject a ticket as out of scope, and a rejection must carry a reason. 
156 
 
ID Rule definition 
BR-25 
Only the customer who raised a ticket may rate it, and only after it reaches Closed. The rating 
window is seven days, during which the customer may instead reopen the ticket; rating it ends 
that option. 
BR-26 
A closed ticket may be reopened by its Customer with a stated reason; reopening restarts the 
workflow at OPEN and starts a new SLA timer. 
BR-27 Messages on the internal thread of a ticket are never visible to the Customer. 
BR-28 
Chat messages on a closed ticket are immutable; only Admin may edit or delete one, and the 
override is audit-logged. 
BR-29 
A maintenance log must be linked to the ticket that produced it and to the asset that was 
serviced. 
BR-30 
A guide article and a blog post keep a version history; publishing creates a new version rather 
than overwriting the previous one. 
BR-31 
Any guide article or blog post can be reused as the starting skeleton for a new one; the platform 
keeps no separate template catalogue. 
BR-32 
An IoT gateway authenticates with its own API key; rotating the key of one device does not 
affect any other device. 
BR-33 
A gateway whose heartbeat stops for longer than the configured window is marked offline and 
raises a device alert. 
BR-34 
A sensor calibration has an expiry date; the platform warns before it lapses so measurement 
accuracy does not drift silently. 
BR-35 
Energy, cost saving and avoided CO2 figures are computed from the regional electricity tariff 
and grid emission factor effective on the day being summarised. 
BR-36 
Every privileged action and every file access is audit-logged with actor, timestamp and outcome, 
and the audit trail is append-only. 
BR-37 
The SLA countdown pauses automatically while a ticket is waiting on information from the 
customer and resumes on the customer's reply, so time spent waiting on the customer is not 
counted against the team. 
BR-38 
Every uploaded file is scanned for malware before it becomes attachable; a file that fails the 
scan is deleted from storage and never reaches a record. 
BR-39 
A chat message passes a content-safety pipeline before it is stored: personally identifiable 
information is detected, profanity is filtered and spam is scored. 
BR-40 
Technical terms and identifiers are masked before a message is translated and restored 
afterwards, so a translated message stays technically correct. 
BR-41 
Third-party data is imported in two passes. The first pass only validates and writes no business 
record; records are written only after the Admin commits the batch explicitly. 
BR-42 
Reverting an import removes the sites, battery assets and devices the batch created, but keeps 
the customer accounts it caused to be issued, because those may already have been used to sign 
in or be attached to a ticket. 
BR-43 
Closing a maintenance ticket starts the next periodic cycle for that battery: the due date is the 
battery asset's next due date, derived from its battery type's maintenance interval, and the ticket 
is raised when that date is reached so the Customer can choose the visit time. Only one periodic 
ticket may exist for the same battery and due date. 
157 
 
ID Rule definition 
BR-44 
Only the owning Customer may choose the visit time of a periodic-maintenance ticket, and only 
while it is still Open and unassigned and within the scheduling window. Once the window closes, 
the Manager arranges the visit instead. 
BR-45 
A gateway device's API key is shown in plaintext only once, when the device is registered; only its 
hash is stored. A device that reaches its daily limit stops receiving messages until the next day. 
BR-46 
Audit entries are read-only: no role, including Admin, may edit or delete one. A record that can 
be altered is not evidence. 
BR-47 
Every notification takes its content from the template of its (type × channel) pair, and exactly one 
version of each pair is active at a time. Editing a template creates a new version rather than 
overwriting the previous one. 
Table 5.1 – Business rules 
5.2 Common Requirements 
ID Rule definition 
CR-01 
All user input is validated on the client and revalidated on the server; the server is the authority 
and never trusts a client-side check. 
CR-02 
Every protected route verifies the session before rendering; while the session is still hydrating a 
loading indicator is shown so the wrong screen never flashes. 
CR-03 
A role mismatch denies access — the web redirects to /unauthorized, the mobile app redirects 
to the login screen — and an unknown web path falls through to the same screen rather than 
rendering blank. 
CR-04 
Real-time data (telemetry, chat, notifications) is pushed to open screens; no screen requires a 
manual refresh to stay current. 
CR-05 
Multi-step flows that do not need a shareable URL (forgot password, reactivate account, 
account settings sections) live on a single route and keep their step in component state. 
CR-06 
Record management screens without a detail view (accounts, roles, battery types, SMS 
gateway) perform create, edit and delete in a dialog over the list rather than on a child route. 
CR-07 Every list that can grow without bound is paginated and filterable on the server side. 
CR-08 Timestamps are stored in UTC and rendered in the viewer's local time zone. 
CR-09 
Any operation that can fail partially across services is expressed as a saga with explicit 
compensation, and its state is inspectable. 
CR-10 
User-facing messages come from the application message list in section 5.3 so wording stays 
consistent across web, mobile and notification channels.

IV. Software Design Document 
1. System Design 
1.1 System Architecture 
The system is deployed as eight independently releasable backend services behind a single API 
gateway, two client applications, an AI inference service and a fleet of edge gateways. Each backend 
service owns its own PostgreSQL schema and never reaches into another service's tables; anything 
that crosses a service boundary is either a synchronous call through the gateway or an asynchronous 
domain event on RabbitMQ. Telemetry is stored in TimescaleDB hypertables, cached lookups in 
Redis, and binary attachments in MinIO. 
Every service is organised in the same four Clean Architecture layers. Domain holds the entities and 
the rules that do not depend on anything outside the service. Application holds the use cases, 
expressed as commands and queries dispatched through a mediator, together with the validation 
and transaction behaviours that wrap them. Infrastructure holds the EF Core persistence, the 
message broker adapters and the external clients. Api holds the controllers, the authentication 
middleware and the composition root. The dependency rule points inwards: Api and Infrastructure 
depend on Application, Application depends on Domain, and Domain depends on nothing. 
Cross-service consistency is handled with the transactional outbox pattern rather than distributed 
transactions. A use case writes its state change and the events it implies in the same database 
transaction; a background dispatcher publishes the outbox rows to RabbitMQ afterwards. Workflows 
that span several services — raising a ticket from an alert, provisioning an account and its profile, 
delivering a notification on three channels — are modelled as sagas with explicit compensation, and 
their state is inspectable from the Saga Debug screen. 
 
162 
 
Figure 1.1 – System architecture: deployment view

packages first, then the two client applications and the edge component. Each figure is followed by a 
table describing the packages it shows.

The backend is ASP.NET Core on .NET 8, split into eight services behind one gateway. Every service

Shared Kernel Libraries 
Three libraries are imported by all eight services. Their dependency order is fixed and visible in the 
figure: SharedKernels and SharedContracts depend on nothing, and SharedInfrastructure imports 
both. Nothing in these libraries depends on a service, which is what keeps the services 
independently releasable. 
174 
 
 
Figure 1.13 – Package diagram: Shared Kernel Libraries 
Package Descriptions 
No Package Description 
01 SharedKernels Domain primitives with no outward dependencies: Domain holds the 
base entity and domain event types, Interfaces holds the generic 
repository and unit-of-work contracts, and Security holds the shared 
authorisation abstractions. 
02 SharedContracts The contract surface exchanged between services, also with no 
dependencies. Common holds shared Requests and Responses; 
Events holds the integration events grouped by area (Audit, Blog, 
Chats, Root); Saga holds the cross-service workflow contracts such as 
AlertTicket; Audit, Interfaces and Protos hold the audit contracts and 
the gRPC definitions. 
03 SharedInfrastructure The only shared library with dependencies: it imports both of the 
above. Behaviors holds the MediatR pipeline behaviours, Bus the 
message broker abstraction, and Caching, Idempotency, Leasing, 
Metrics, Middleware and RateLimiting the cross-cutting concerns. 
Persistence holds the shared Interceptors and Repositories, and 
DependencyInjection, Extensions, Services and Swagger complete the 
library.

BatteryService calls the AI module through IAiPredictionClient and caches the result against the 
asset, so opening a screen never triggers an inference. The table below is the contract the clients

176 
 
Degraded behaviour is part of the contract rather than an afterthought. When the AI module does 
not answer, the clients show the last cached prediction together with its age and MSG-ERR-19 is 
recorded; threshold-based alerting and the whole ticket workflow continue unaffected, because 
nothing on the safety path waits for an inference. 
1.4 AI Module — Consumed Interface 
The AI module is a separate FastAPI service. This document specifies only what the rest of the system 
consumes from it — the endpoints and the fields the screens render. The model architecture, feature 
engineering and training pipeline are internal to that service and are specified in its own 
documentation, not here. 
No Endpoint / package What the clients consume 
01 GET /health 
Readiness of each model artefact. The backend uses it to decide whether 
a health estimate can be refreshed at all; when it reports a model as not 
loaded, the clients keep showing the last cached value with its age. 
02 POST /predict 
The only endpoint on the critical path. Given a window of readings for one 
asset it returns the state-of-health percentage, the anomaly class (Normal, 
Degrading or Failed) and a confidence score, plus the model version and 
the measured latency. BatteryService calls it through IAiPredictionClient 
and caches the result. 
03 POST /verify 
Compares a prediction against a labelled sample. Used during evaluation, 
not by the clients. 
04 POST /prescribe 
Planned for Sprint 3 and not yet built. The backend already holds the 
client interface (IAiPrescriptionClient), so no screen depends on it today. 
05 
src/models, 
src/features, 
src/services, src/core 
Model artefacts, feature engineering and the loading pipeline. Internal to 
the AI service — no client addresses them directly, and this document 
does not specify them. 
Table 1.15 – AI module interface consumed by the platform 
2. Database Design 
Each service owns its own schema and no service reads another service's tables. Where a record 
must point at something owned elsewhere it stores an external reference — the identifier alone — 
and resolves it through the owning service's API or through a locally maintained read model kept 
current by domain events. That is why BatteryService holds a Customer table and TicketService holds 
CustomerAccount and StaffAccount tables: they are projections synchronised from AuthService, not 
copies maintained by hand. 
The telemetry tables are the exception to ordinary relational sizing. SensorReading and 
AmbientReading are TimescaleDB hypertables, append-only and partitioned by time, because they 
grow at a fixed rate per device forever. Nothing updates or deletes a reading; a measurement 
correction is expressed as a calibration record, and analytics read from pre-computed daily 
summaries rather than scanning the raw stream. 
The diagrams below are generated from the live schemas rather than drawn by hand, so every 
column, type, primary key and foreign key shown is the one the database actually enforces. One 
177 
 
diagram is given per database, because a service never joins across a boundary and a single 
combined picture would imply relationships that do not exist. 
2.1 AuthService — auth_db 
Identity and authorisation: the account and its profiles, the role and permission matrix, and the 
session tables that hold refresh tokens, trusted devices, backup codes and the sign-in attempt log. 
 
Figure 2.1 – Database schema: AuthService — auth_db 
2.2 BatteryService — battery_db 
Assets and telemetry: sites, battery types and their versioned thresholds, the battery assets 
themselves, the append-only sensor and ambient readings, the alert and incident tables, the cached 
AI results and the IoT device registry. 
178 
 
 
Figure 2.2 – Database schema: BatteryService — battery_db 
2.3 TicketService — ticket_db 
Tickets and everything attached to them: assignments, participants, the activity timeline, SLA timers 
and their pause events, the chat tables with reads, reactions, mentions and translations, the Guide 
and blog content with their version history, and the saga state tables. 
179 
 
 
Figure 2.3 – Database schema: TicketService — ticket_db 
2.4 NotificationService — notification_db 
Delivery and addressing: the notification records and the templates they are rendered from, 
per-account and per-category preferences, registered device tokens, push receipts, and the batch 
and group tables used for a broadcast. 
180 
 
 
Figure 2.4 – Database schema: NotificationService — notification_db 
SmsService, FileStorageService and AuditAggregatorService are not shown as diagrams. Their 
schemas are small and almost entirely free-standing — a gateway device with its outbound message 
queue, the object metadata with its access log, and the aggregated audit trail — so they are covered 
by the table descriptions below instead. 
2.5 Files Description 
Not everything the system stores is a database row. Binary content — profile photographs, ticket 
attachments, maintenance evidence, guide images and firmware images — is held in S3-compatible 
object storage (MinIO), and the database keeps only the metadata needed to find and authorise it. 
The bytes and the row describing them are therefore two separate things, which is what allows an 
attachment to be served directly to a browser without passing through a service. 
No Property Value 
01 Provider MinIO, addressed through the S3 API with path-style addressing, so the same 
code runs against any S3-compatible endpoint without change. 
181 
 
No Property Value 
02 Bucket solar-battery-files — a single bucket for the whole platform. It is created at 
startup if absent, and the deployment applies an anonymous download 
policy so a stored object can be fetched directly by URL. 
03 Object key <folder>/<32-character GUID><extension>, for example 
ticket-attachments/9f1c4b7e2a8d4f6b9c0e5a3d7b2f8e14.pdf. The original 
file name is never used as the key; it is preserved as object metadata and in 
the database row instead. 
04 Folder segment Supplied per upload and sanitised before use: invalid path characters are 
replaced, any '..' sequence is neutralised, and an empty value falls back to 
'default'. This is what prevents a crafted folder name from escaping the 
bucket prefix. 
05 Maximum size 20 MB per file (MaxFileSizeBytes). An empty upload is rejected outright, and 
a file with no extension is rejected before it reaches storage. 
06 Allowed extensions .jpg, .jpeg, .png, .webp, .gif — images; .pdf, .doc, .docx, .xls, .xlsx, .txt, .csv — 
documents; .bin, .hex, .fw — firmware images for the IoT rollout path. 
07 Content type Derived from the file's own signature bytes rather than from the 
Content-Type the client declares, then stored with the object. A mis-declared 
header would otherwise make an image or document fail to open when 
served directly from the bucket. 
08 Access Reads are served either by a presigned URL with a bounded lifetime or 
through the service, which checks the caller against the file's purpose before 
streaming it. Every upload and download is written to file_audit_logs with 
the actor and the outcome. 
Table 2.1 – Object storage configuration 
Uploads are separated by purpose, and the purpose is what decides both the folder an object lands 
in and who may read it afterwards. 
No Property Content held 
01 Avatar Profile photographs for an account. 
02 TicketAttachment Files attached to a ticket or posted into a ticket chat thread. These are the 
only uploads that also pass through the virus scan worker, which records its 
verdict on the attachment row. 
03 MaintenancePhoto Before and after evidence captured by a technician while resolving a ticket, 
referenced from the maintenance log. 
04 KbImage Images embedded in a guide article or a blog post. 
05 Firmware Firmware images published for the IoT gateways, verified by checksum before 
a device applies one. 
06 Other Anything that does not fall into the categories above. 
Table 2.2 – Storage folders by file purpose 
Every stored object carries a status, so a file that failed a check is withheld rather than silently 
deleted. 
182 
 
No Status Meaning 
01 Uploaded The object is written to storage but not yet usable. 
02 Processing Post-upload work is in progress, such as a scan. 
03 Ready The object may be served. This is the normal state. 
04 Quarantined Withheld from every reader after a scan verdict; the row is kept so the event 
stays auditable rather than disappearing. 
05 Deleted Soft-deleted. The row remains for the audit trail after the object itself has 
been removed. 
Table 2.3 – Stored object lifecycle states 
2.6 Table Descriptions 
Every table in the system is listed below with its purpose, its primary key and its foreign keys. All 
tables use a single surrogate primary key named id, except audit_aggregate, which is keyed by id and 
occurred_at so the trail can be partitioned by time. Where a column identifies a row owned by a 
different service it is listed as an external reference rather than a foreign key: the value is resolved 
through that service's API or through a local read model, and no database constraint enforces it, 
because a foreign key cannot span two databases.

Table 2.4 – Table descriptions 
3. Detailed Design 
Each subsection below covers one feature: the class diagrams of the participants in its main flows, 
followed by a specification of every class in them. The pipeline is the same throughout — a controller 
forwards a command to the mediator, a validation behaviour runs before the handler, the handler 
works through repository interfaces, and the unit of work commits the state change together with 
the outbox rows it produced. 
3.1 Account Registration and Activation 
Owning service: AuthService 
Registration creates an inactive account and defers activation to an emailed one-time code. The 
password is hashed with BCrypt inside the handler and the plaintext never leaves the request scope. 
188 
 
The verification email is not sent inline: the handler writes the integration event to the outbox in the 
same transaction as the account row, so an account is never created without its email being queued 
and never has an email queued without being created.

en)

what guarantees a state change and the event it implies cannot 
diverge.

Explicit transaction control for a use case that must span 
several repositories. 
ISystemClock 
No Method Description 
01 UtcNow 
The single source of time in the service. Injecting it is what 
makes expiry rules such as code validity and token lifetime 
testable.

3.2 Sign-in, Two-Factor and Token Issue 
Owning service: AuthService 
Sign-in verifies the password, records the attempt, and only then issues tokens. Two rules are 
enforced on the server rather than in the client: an account locks once consecutive failures reach the 
limit, and the platform a role may use is checked before the session is released — a Customer is 
refused on the web and an Admin or Manager on mobile, with the tokens that were just minted 
discarded. Two-factor is TOTP-based, with an SMS one-time code and single-use backup codes as 
alternatives, and a cross-device confirmation path for approving a sign-in from an already trusted 
device.

3.3 Battery Asset and Threshold Management 
Owning service: BatteryService 
Thresholds are versioned rather than edited. Activating a new version leaves the previous one intact, 
which is what lets an alert raised months ago still be interpreted against the limits in force when it 
fired. Asset registration enforces the immutability of the serial number and the requirement that an 
asset belongs to exactly one site and one battery type. 
202

3.4 Telemetry Ingestion 
Owning service: BatteryService 
Ingestion is the highest-volume path and the one that must neither lose nor double-count data. 
Gateways publish over MQTT, which the platform bridges into the ingestion pipeline; an 
authenticated HTTP path exists for the same payload. Each batch carries an idempotency key, so a 
gateway replaying its offline buffer is safe. Accepted readings are appended to a TimescaleDB 
hypertable, pushed to connected clients over SignalR, and evaluated against the active threshold 
version.

3.5 Alert Generation, Escalation and Auto-resolution 
Owning service: BatteryService 
The alerting design is dominated by noise control. Repeats of the same anomaly on one asset merge 
into the single open alert; five or more assets breaching identically at one site collapse into a site 
alert; and a value oscillating around a limit is recorded as a noise breach event rather than alerting. 
What does escalate is silence: an alert nobody acknowledges climbs a fixed ladder and opens a P1 
ticket at the top of it. An alert whose underlying condition has cleared can also be resolved 
automatically rather than waiting for a human.

3.6 AI Prediction — Client Integration 
Owning service: BatteryService 
220 
 
This section specifies only how the platform consumes the AI service; the model architecture and 
training pipeline belong to the AI module's own documentation. The call is deliberately kept off the 
critical path: threshold alerting does not wait for a prediction, and a prediction that cannot be 
obtained blocks nothing — the last cached value is served with its age and MSG-ERR-19 is recorded. 
Results are cached against the asset with their confidence, model version and latency, so opening a 
screen never triggers an inference.

Degraded-path behaviour 
No Method Description 
01 Cached value with age 
When PredictAsync returns null the clients render the last 
stored prediction together with predictedAt, so a stale estimate 
is never presented as current. 
02 Threshold alerting unaffected 
Alert generation reads thresholds, not predictions, so the 
safety path keeps working while the AI service is down. 
03 No ticket on AI grounds alone 
With no fresh classification the automatic ticket is not opened; 
a threshold breach still opens one on its own merits.

3.7 Ticket Lifecycle, Priority and SLA 
Owning service: TicketService 
The state machine is a first-class component rather than logic scattered through handlers: 
ITicketStateMachine validates every transition against the target state, the actor's role and the 
actor's identity, so an invalid or unauthorised transition is refused wherever it is attempted. Priority is 
not typed in by hand — IPriorityCalculator derives it from the impact scope and urgency the manager 
selects, which is the ITIL matrix, and the derived priority then fixes the deadline. The SLA can pause, 
and it pauses automatically while the ticket is waiting on the customer.

3.8 Ticket Chat and Content Safety 
Owning service: TicketService 
The chat carries two threads on one ticket, and the internal thread is filtered by 
IChatAuthorizationService rather than in the client, so a customer session cannot retrieve it by 
manipulating a request. Beyond delivery, the composer runs a content-safety pipeline before a 
message is stored: personally identifiable information is detected, profanity is filtered, spam is scored 
and technical terms are masked before translation so they are not mangled. Voice messages are 
transcribed rather than stored as opaque audio. 
3.8.1 Class Diagrams and Sequence Diagrams 
Chat Add 
 
 
Figure 3.57 – Class diagram: Chat Add

3.9 Notification Dispatch 
Owning service: NotificationService 
Dispatch is fan-out with per-recipient routing. Content is rendered once from a stored template and 
then delivered on whichever channels the recipient has enabled, each channel implementing the 
same INotificationChannel contract so a dead SMS gateway cannot stop a push notification. Mobile 
push goes through Expo's push service.

3.10 File Storage, Virus Scanning and Access Control 
Owning service: FileStorageService 
Files never travel through the domain services. A client transfers bytes against a presigned reference 
and the domain service only ever holds the identifier. Every upload is scanned for malware before it 
becomes attachable, and authorisation for a download is resolved against the record the file is 
attached to rather than against the file itself.

3.11 Cross-service Consistency: Outbox, Saga and Audit 
Owning service: Shared Infrastructure 
This section describes the mechanism the rest of the design depends on. No use case publishes an 
event inline: events are written to the outbox in the same transaction as the state change and 
relayed afterwards, so a crash between the two is impossible. Workflows that span services are sagas 
whose state is what the Saga Debug screen renders, and each service emits its audit records through 
the same outbox mechanism for the aggregator to collect.

3.13 Third-party Data Import 
Owning service: BatteryService (with AuthService and EmailService) 
307 
 
Import runs in two phases with a hard boundary between them: uploading a file parses and validates 
it but writes no business data, so an Admin always sees the outcome before anything is committed. 
CreateImportBatchCommandHandler collects every error on a row instead of stopping at the first, 
and refuses a re-upload of the same content by SHA-256 of the bytes ratherthan by file name. 
Commit only enqueues work; ImportBatchProcessorBackgroundService advances a batch one stage 
per pass in the order customers, sites, assets, because a site needs its customer and an asset needs 
its site. Customer accounts are provisioned across services through the outbox and the message bus, 
so a batch legitimately sits in AwaitingAccountSync until AuthService answers, and a row still 
unanswered at the configured deadline is failed rather than left hanging. Revert removes only the 
records the batch created, in reverse order, and deliberately leaves the customer accounts alone 
because they may already have been used to sign in.

3.14 AI Module – Internal Design and Special Diagrams 
Owning component: ai-module (Python 3.11) 
Section 3.6 specified only the contract the platform consumes. This section documents the module 
behind that contract: how the service is assembled, how a battery window is turned into a 
57-dimensional feature vector, what the network actually computes, and which decision rules turn a 
raw state-of-health figure into the risk profile the web and mobile clients display. Every diagram is 
derived from the code in the ai-module repository, so it can be re-verified whenever that code 
changes. 
Two properties shape the whole design. The module is stateless — it owns no relational database, 
only an in-memory state-of-health history keyed by battery — so any instance can serve any request 
and a restart loses nothing the platform cannot resend. And it exposes one pipeline through two 
transports: the production gRPC server on port 50051 and a FastAPI REST surface kept for 
development and manual testing. Both validate against the same Pydantic schemas and both reach 
the same service layer, so the two surfaces cannot drift apart. 
3.14.1 Component Structure and Data Preparation 
AI Module Component Diagram 
The component diagram reads left to right through four layers. BatteryService and TicketService call 
the transport layer, where the gRPC server publishes the ten procedures of AiService and the FastAPI 
surface is drawn with a dashed connector to mark it as the non-production path. Everything then 
passes through one validation component — the Pydantic schemas in src/schemas — before entering 
the service layer, which holds inference, prescription, verify and suggest. On the right sits the state 
the module depends on: the RAG vector store built from the knowledge directory, two complete 
artifact sets held side by side (NASA/NMC v1.6 and LFP v2.2-lfp), and the in-memory battery_history 
component. The only external dependency is the LLM provider chain, reached from the prescription 
component alone. 
Two points are worth making from this figure in a review. First, each artifact set is a matched triple — 
checkpoint, scaler and Isolation Forest — and the set is chosen from the requested chemistry before 
any transformation runs, so a scaler from one set can never be paired with a checkpoint from the 
other. Second, port 50051 is not published outside the Docker network; the platform reaches it only 
from inside. 
 
318 
 
Figure 3.120 – Component diagram: AI module inside the system 
Training Data Flow 
The data-flow diagram covers the offline half of the module. Raw NASA Ames .mat files and the 
Severson and SNL .csv sets are loaded into discharge cycles, split by battery identity into 24 training 
batteries, one validation and one test battery, and only then is the MinMaxScaler fitted — on training 
cycles alone, and written out as scaler.pkl. Windows of 30 timesteps at stride 30 are sliced, the 
derived cycle-count and state-of-charge columns are appended, the 57 window features are 
extracted, and a second scaler is fitted on the training features only. The state-of-health label is the 
measured capacity divided by nominal capacity. Splitting by battery rather than by window is what 
keeps cycles of the same cell out of both sides of the split, and fitting both scalers on the training 
side alone is what keeps the test figures honest. 
 
Figure 3.121 – Data flow diagram: dataset preparation and artifact generation 
319 
 
3.14.2 Model Design 
Feature Engineering – the 57-dimensional Vector 
Feature extraction takes the scaled window and keeps its first three channels — voltage, current and 
temperature. Each channel yields ten spectral features and nine statistical features, concatenated in 
a fixed order into a 57-value vector, which is then passed through the feature scaler. The resulting 
vector serves two consumers at once: the Isolation Forest scores it through decision_function, and 
the same vector conditions the neural network through its FiLM layer. Scoring the feature vector 
rather than the raw sequence is deliberate — the Isolation Forest was fitted on exactly this 
distribution, and the fixed concatenation order is what lets a stored forest keep matching a freshly 
extracted vector. 
 
Figure 3.122 – Feature engineering: construction of the 57-dimensional vector 
Neural Network Architecture 
The figure shows the two configurations of the predictor side by side; they are distinct networks and 
should not be read as one. The production path, on the left, projects the (30, 6) window to 64 
dimensions, passes it through two Mamba blocks (d_model 64, d_state 16, d_conv 4, expand 2), 
normalises and pools by taking the last token. The centre column is the conditioning branch: the 57 
cycle-level features go through a small projection with a SiLU activation, are expanded to 128 values 
and split into a scale and a shift, which modulate the pooled hidden state. A two-layer head with 
GELU and dropout then produces the state-of-health value in the unit interval. 
The right-hand column is the long-sequence variant used by PredictLong. It embeds a 4096-step 
input with a strided convolution into 256 tokens, adds per-patch degradation statistics, uses a wider 
state dimension and pools by multi-head attention with a discharge-phase bias before reaching the 
320 
 
same conditioning and head. Both configurations are written in pure PyTorch, so no mamba-ssm 
CUDA extension is required and the model runs natively on Windows; an opt-in flag substitutes the 
CUDA kernel on Kaggle or Colab with identical mathematics. 
 
321 
 
322 
 
Figure 3.123 – Neural network architecture: production and long-sequence configurations 
3.14.3 Pipeline Activity Diagrams 
Training Pipeline 
Training is deterministic by seed and refuses to start when the feature-scaler version does not match 
the one recorded with the tensors — the guard against training a checkpoint against features it will 
never see again at inference time. The loop uses Adam with a weighted mean-squared error, keeps 
the best validation checkpoint, reduces the learning rate on plateau and stops on patience or at the 
epoch cap. Only after the test evaluation are the checkpoint and the matching Isolation Forest 
written out, and they are written as a versioned pair so the two can never be committed out of step. 
 
323 
 
324 
 
Figure 3.124 – Activity diagram: training pipeline 
Inference Pipeline 
At inference the artifact set is resolved first, from the chemistry in the request. The pack is then 
converted to cell terms — voltage divided by the series count, current rescaled by the 
nominal-to-actual capacity ratio — before the scaler runs, so an eight-series 24-volt pack is scored 
against the per-cell distribution the model was trained on. From the scaled window the pipeline 
forks: two derived columns are appended for the sequence model, while the 57 features are 
extracted and scaled for the Isolation Forest and the conditioning branch. 
Monte-Carlo dropout then draws ten stochastic samples in a single batched forward pass. The mean 
is reported as the state-of-health, the standard deviation becomes the confidence, and the median 
drives every threshold because it resists outliers at ten samples. The remaining activities run from 
those aggregates: the causal degradation rate is computed against the battery's own recent cycles 
held in battery_history, which is also where the new point is recorded; the anomaly tier, the health 
stage and the sensor status are classified; warnings, degradation metrics and the temperature 
domain distance are computed; and everything is combined into the risk profile carried by the 
response. The whole pipeline is budgeted under 100 ms. 
 
325 
 
Figure 3.125 – Activity diagram: inference pipeline inside run_inference() 
Classification Decision Logic 
This is the part reviewers ask about most, because it is where a continuous number becomes a 
discrete verdict shown to a technician. Two independent ladders run. The anomaly score is banded 
into Anomaly, Warning or Normal at −0.3 and −0.1. The state-of-health is banded into Failed below 
80 %, Degrading below 90 %, and below that threshold the anomaly score still decides between 
Degrading and Normal. 
Two refinements sit on top. The causal degradation rate — the fade measured against this battery's 
own last two recorded cycles rather than against a fleet average — escalates the tier by one step 
when it exceeds 0.5016 %SOH per cycle, a threshold taken as the 90th percentile of the training 
distribution rather than picked by hand. In parallel, every Monte-Carlo sample is binned at the 80 % 
line and the health stage is the argmax of those shares, with ties broken towards the more severe 
outcome, so an uncertain prediction is never rounded in the optimistic direction. The three results 
are combined into the risk profile — risk level, priority and action code — that the clients render. 
 
326 
 
Figure 3.126 – Activity diagram: classification decision logic 
Prescription and RAG Pipeline 
Prescribe runs inference first, then always builds a rule-based baseline, so a prescription exists even 
when no language model is reachable. When enrichment is requested, the diagnosis statement is 
expanded into three to five retrieval queries — by the model when it is available, from templates 
otherwise — the maintenance and safety collections are retrieved per query and de-duplicated by 
chunk identity under a cap, and previously accepted prescriptions for similar cases are pulled from 
the history store as context before the model generates the draft. 
Every path, enriched or not, converges on the same safety gate covering personal protective 
equipment, lockout, thermal handling and a blocklist, followed by a model-as-judge verdict. A 
blocked draft is audited, replaced by the rule-based text and put through the gate again — the 
replacement is never trusted merely because it came from the rules. Whatever is finally delivered is 
written to the history store, which is what makes the feedback loop below possible. 
 
327 
 
Figure 3.127 – Activity diagram: prescription and RAG pipeline 
3.14.4 Integration Sequences 
gRPC AiService – Predict and Prescribe 
The sequence diagram traces the two calls the platform actually depends on. In the Predict half, 
BatteryService builds the request, the servicer validates it against the Pydantic schema and answers a 
violation with INVALID_ARGUMENT before any model is touched; a valid request resolves its artifact 
set by chemistry, reads and then records the battery's own history for the causal rate, and returns 
the prediction, anomaly, risk and evidence in one response. 
328 
 
In the Prescribe half, TicketService calls the orchestrator, which reuses the same inference call rather 
than a second code path, builds the rule-based baseline, and only then — and only when enrichment 
is requested — involves the retriever and the model chain. The safety gate is drawn outside that 
optional block on purpose: it runs on every response, and the fallback to rule-based text is 
re-validated rather than returned directly. The prescription is saved and its identifier returned, which 
is what the feedback call in the next figure refers to. 
 
Figure 3.128 – Sequence diagram: Predict and Prescribe over gRPC 
AI Feedback – Classification and Prescription 
Two feedback channels close the loop. Grading a classification appends one line to the feedback log 
under a lock and returns the running accuracy statistics, which is what the evaluation dashboard 
reports. Grading a prescription updates the record in the history store; when the identifier does not 
resolve to a stored prescription the call is refused with NOT_FOUND, mirroring the 404 of the REST 
route, so the ticket service never silently discards a technician's verdict. 
329 
 
The effect is not immediate retraining. Accepted prescriptions are retrieved as past cases and 
supplied as context on later requests, so the system improves within the current model rather than 
waiting for the next one to be trained. 
 
Figure 3.129 – Sequence diagram: classification and prescription feedback 
AiService gRPC Surface 
The complete set of remote procedures exposed on port 50051 is listed below. Every procedure 
mirrors a REST route of the same name, and the two share the request and response schemas. 
No RPC Description 
01 Predict 
Scores one 30-step window: state-of-health with confidence, 
anomaly score, health stage, warnings and the combined risk 
profile. 
330 
 
No RPC Description 
02 PredictStream 
Bidirectional streaming form of Predict, used when a batch of 
assets is scored in one connection. 
03 PredictLong 
Long-sequence variant (L ≤ 4096) using the attention-pooling 
model: one forward pass, no Monte-Carlo dropout and no 
anomaly score, because the Isolation Forest was fitted only on 
window-30 features. 
04 Prescribe 
Returns a maintenance prescription: rule-based baseline, 
optionally enriched by retrieval and a language model, always 
passed through the safety gate. 
05 VerifyTicket 
Scores a manually raised ticket as genuine or noise and detects 
descriptions duplicating an existing ticket. 
06 SuggestStaff 
Ranks technicians for a ticket and states the reason for each; 
the manager still makes the assignment. 
07 SuggestKb 
Ranks knowledge-base articles to consult during the repair; a 
reference is recorded only when the technician applies one. 
08 SubmitFeedback 
Records a technician's verdict on a delivered prescription 
(accepted, edited or rejected) together with the edited steps; 
an unknown prescription identifier is answered with 
NOT_FOUND. 
09 SubmitClassificationFeedback 
Records a verdict on an AI classification and returns the 
running accuracy statistics. 
10 Health 
Liveness probe reporting the loaded artifact versions and 
readiness. 
 
3.15 IoT Firmware – Node Design and Special Diagrams 
Owning component: iot/firmware-esp32 (PlatformIO, Arduino-ESP32 on FreeRTOS) 
The gateway node is the only part of the system that runs outside the data centre, with no operator 
at the keyboard and no guarantee of connectivity. Its design is therefore dominated by two concerns 
that do not arise elsewhere in this document: it must keep measuring while the network is down, 
and it must never write the same reading twice when the network returns. The four diagrams below 
describe the node from four angles — the system block diagram, the wiring of the board, the 
electrical schematic, and the data flow through the firmware. 
One structural decision underpins all of them. Every rule that can be decided without touching 
hardware — filtering a reading, choosing a transport, shaping a payload, deciding whether a failure is 
worth retrying — lives in core/, a layer that calls no Arduino or ESP-IDF function. It is the block 
labelled Processing (core) in the block diagram, and it is compiled and unit-tested on the 
development machine through the PlatformIO env:native environment, so the logic is covered by 
tests that need no board attached. 
331 
 
3.15.1 Node and Firmware Diagrams 
Block Diagram 
The block diagram separates the field side from the gateway pipeline and the platform behind it. In 
the field are the LiFePO4 bank in a 24-series configuration, a 0.375 mΩ shunt inserted in the main 
current path, the JK-BD6A24S10P battery management units 1..N read over UART Modbus, an 
INA226 providing a redundant voltage and current measurement, DS18B20 probes for per-battery 
temperature, and the MQ-2 smoke and water-leak sensors that raise environmental incidents. The 
power subsystem — a 5 V adapter or a 12 V supply through an LM2596 buck, backed by a TP4056 
and 18650 cell, with a 3V3 LDO and a common ground — sits alongside them. 
The gateway pipeline runs top to bottom in five stages: acquisition over Modbus RTU, I²C, 1-Wire and 
the ADC/GPIO inputs; processing in core/, where reading_filter, ingest_policy and the payload builder 
attach the idempotency key; a local LittleFS queue that provides store-and-forward with exponential 
backoff; connectivity, holding the Wi-Fi, MQTT and HTTPS clients together with TLS and NTP; and 
device management, which owns provisioning and identity in NVS, the serial CLI and the status LED. 
A provisioning web page served on the node's own Wi-Fi address feeds that last stage over SoftAP, a 
QR code or Wi-Fi. On the platform side, telemetry travels over MQTT with TLS to a Mosquitto or 
EMQX broker bridged into the backend microservices, ambient, incident and heartbeat events travel 
over HTTPS directly, results are persisted through TimescaleDB, Redis and RabbitMQ, and analytics 
read from there. Commands travel back to the node over MQTT. 
 
Figure 3.130 – Block diagram: IoT node, gateway pipeline and platform 
Wiring Diagram 
The wiring diagram fixes the pin assignment. GPIO17, GPIO18 and GPIO16 drive the DI, RO and 
DE/RE lines of the 3V3 UART transceiver, whose A/B twisted pair reaches the JK-BMS units at 9600 
baud with a 120 Ω terminator at each end of the bus. GPIO4 carries the 1-Wire line to the DS18B20 
probes with a 4.7 kΩ pull-up to 3V3. GPIO1, on ADC1, reads the MQ-2 analogue output after a 
divider brings its 5 V swing down to 3.3 V, and GPIO2 reads the water-leak sensor as a digital input. 
GPIO48 drives the on-board WS2812 status LED, and the on-chip radio carries both uplinks. UART0 
doubles as the USB serial console at 115200 baud. 
Two branches are drawn dashed because they are provisioned but unused: the SPI card slot, since 
the firmware keeps its queue on the internal flash through LittleFS, and the SN65HVD230 CAN 
332 
 
transceiver, since the battery management units are read over UART rather than CAN. The supply 
accepts a 5 V/3 A adapter, a fused 12 V line through the LM2596 buck, or the TP4056 and 18650 
uninterruptible path boosted back to 5 V; the board's own LDO then feeds module VCC at 3V3, with 
470–1000 µF of bulk capacitance placed near the ESP32. The single most common wiring fault is 
addressed explicitly by the diagram: the ESP32, every module and the battery management units 
must share one common ground rail. 
 
Figure 3.131 – Wiring diagram: pin assignment and power distribution 
Schematic 
The schematic redraws the same node electrically, against the +5V, +3V3 and GND rails. The shunt 
sits between the battery positive terminal and the load, and its IN+ and IN− taps feed the INA226 at 
I²C address 0x40, whose SDA and SCL lines run to GPIO8 and GPIO9 with a 4.7 kΩ pull-up on each. 
The DS18B20 chain on GPIO4 has its own 4.7 kΩ 1-Wire pull-up, the MQ-2 analogue output reaches 
GPIO1 through the resistor divider, and the water-leak sensor drives GPIO2 directly. The UART 
transceiver runs from 3V3 on GPIO17/18/16 with the two 120 Ω A/B terminators, the WS2812 sits on 
GPIO48, and the 470–1000 µF decoupling capacitor bridges the 5 V rail to ground next to the power 
input. 
333 
 
 
Figure 3.132 – Schematic: power rails, sensing chain and bus terminations 
Data Flow Diagram 
The data-flow diagram states what the firmware does with a reading rather than how it is wired. Four 
external entities appear: the battery management units, the sensor group, the provisioning 
application or an operator on the serial console, and the backend cloud. Readings are acquired (1.0), 
then filtered, validated and built into a payload carrying its idempotency key (2.0). From there the 
validated batch either goes out immediately or is enqueued for retry under exponential backoff (3.0) 
against the local LittleFS store (D1); telemetry is published over MQTT with TLS (4.0), while ambient, 
incident and heartbeat events are posted over HTTPS (5.0). 
The key that is generated once in 2.0 travels with the record into D1, which is what makes a replay 
after reconnection safe: the backend recognises the repeat and does not write a second row. The 
remaining two processes close the control loop. Provisioning and identity (8.0) obtains the device 
credentials and synchronises time over NTP, writing both into the NVS store (D2); commands arriving 
on the MQTT subscription (6.0) are applied and written back to D2 as a configuration update, which 
acquisition and publication then read on their next pass. 
 
Figure 3.133 – Data flow diagram: firmware processes, stores and external entities 
334 
 
3.15.2 Device Communication Surface 
The complete set of endpoints and topics a node uses is listed below; nothing else is reachable from 
the field. 
No Topic / endpoint Description 
01 
solar/{deviceCode}/{batterySerial}/
telemetry 
MQTT publish — one message per battery per poll tick, 
best-effort delivery. 
02 solar/{deviceCode}/heartbeat 
MQTT publish — periodic liveness signal at the interval 
returned by provisioning. 
03 solar/{deviceCode}/status 
MQTT publish — retained online marker, with the matching 
offline marker set as the broker's last will so an abrupt 
disconnect is still reported.

MQTT subscribe — the only inbound channel: polling interval,

MQTT publish — acknowledgement carrying the command 
identifier, a status and, on failure, the reason.

HTTPS — one-time handshake returning the polling and

broker credentials. 
07 POST /api/iot-devices/heartbeat 
HTTPS — heartbeat used as the authoritative reachability 
check; its acknowledgement is what turns the status LED 
green. 
08 POST /api/sensor-readings/batch 
HTTPS — batch ingest carrying an idempotency key; used as 
the MQTT fallback and as the only path for draining the offline 
queue. 
09 POST /api/ambient/readings/batch HTTPS — ambient temperature and humidity readings. 
10 POST /api/environmental-incidents 
HTTPS — smoke and water-leak incidents; sampled and latched 
even while offline so an event cannot be lost with the network.

V. Software Test Documentation 
1. Scope of Testing 
1.1 Purpose 
The purpose of this testing effort is to verify that all implemented features of the Solar Lithium-ion 
Battery Maintenance Management System function correctly, meet business requirements, and 
provide a stable, reliable experience for end users (Customers, Staff, Managers, and Administrators). 
1.2 In-scope features 
● Authentication & Identity Management (AuthService) 
● User registration & Email OTP verification 
● Login / Logout (including Google login and 2FA) 
● Forgot password / Reset password 
● Session handling, token refresh, and role-based access control 
● Profile management, trusted device management, audit logs 
● Battery & Site Management (BatteryService) 
● Site and battery asset creation & management 
● Sensor data ingestion (realtime and historical) 
● Alert generation based on SOH / temperature thresholds 
● Environmental incident handling 
● Dashboard statistics and reports 
● AI Module (SOH / RUL / Anomaly Detection) 
● SOH prediction accuracy and response latency 
● Anomaly / degrading pattern detection 
● Integration and fallback resilience with BatteryService 
● Ticket & SLA Management (TicketService) 
● Ticket creation (from alert or manual), assignment, and SLA auto-escalation 
● Staff processing workflow (start, hold, resume, resolve) 
● Chat/collaboration on tickets, guide suggestions 
● CSAT and staff performance reports 
● Notification & Multi-Channel Alerts (NotificationService / EmailService / SmsService) 
● In-app and push notifications 
● Notification preferences and unsubscribe 
● SMS channel and OTP delivery 
● Admin templates, preview, test send, and broadcast 
● File Storage (FileStorageService) 
● File upload/download, presigned URL, metadata 
336 
 
● Access-permission checks between users 
● Web Application (Frontend – ReactJS) 
● Role-based navigation for Admin, Manager, Staff 
● Responsive layout and cross-browser compatibility (Chrome, Edge) 
● Mobile Application (React Native / Expo) 
● Auth flows, Customer and Staff tab experiences 
● Offline/connectivity handling and data sync 
● General Functional Requirements 
● Navigation across pages, form validation 
● Error messages and edge cases 
● Role permissions 
● Non-functional Requirements 
● Basic performance (page load, responsiveness) 
● Usability (clear UI, intuitive workflow) 
● Compatibility on major browsers (Chrome, Edge) 
1.3 Out-of-scope features 
● Third-party API internal logic (internal processing of MQTT broker, SMS/Email gateway providers) 
● Performance testing at scale (load/stress/DDoS simulation excluded due to project limits) 
● Security penetration testing (only basic security checks – login restrictions, form validation, RBAC 
– will be done) 
1.4 Testing levels included 
● Unit Testing – Verifying functions, components and modules 
● Integration Testing – Ensuring modules and services work together 
● System Testing – Validating end-to-end workflows 
● Acceptance Testing – Stakeholder verification 
1.5 Assumptions 
● Stable development environment will be available during testing 
● All features to be tested are deployed on a test server 
● Test data (sample sites, batteries, sensor readings) will be provided 
● Staff / Manager / Admin roles will be accessible for testing 
1.6 Constraints 
● Limited time for full non-functional testing 
● Small QA team (students) limits coverage of deep security testing 
● Some functionality depends on backend/service readiness 
337 
 
2. Test Strategy 
2.1 Testing Types 
Functional Testing 
Objective: Verify that each function of the system works according to the specified requirements. 
Technique: 
● Requirement-based testing 
● Positive & negative test scenarios 
● Input validation 
● Data-driven testing 
Completion Criteria: 
● All functional test cases executed 
● 100% of critical functions passed 
● No high-severity defects remain 
UI/UX Testing 
Objective: Ensure the interface is clear, user-friendly, consistent, and follows design standards. 
Technique: 
● UI behavior verification 
● Layout and alignment checks 
● Figma-to-UI comparison 
● Usability walkthrough from user perspective 
Completion Criteria: 
● All pages follow the Figma design 
● No major UI inconsistencies 
● All input components are accessible and responsive 
Validation & Error Handling Testing 
Objective: Verify the system properly handles invalid inputs and displays correct error messages. 
Technique: 
● Boundary value testing 
● Negative testing 
● Form validation 
● Error-state testing 
Completion Criteria: 
● All forms show proper validation messages 
338 
 
● No unhandled exceptions or frontend crashes 
Security/Access Control Testing 
Objective: Confirm users can only access authorized areas and sensitive operations are protected. 
Technique: 
● Authentication checks 
● Authorization tests (RBAC) 
● Session expiration testing 
● Brute-force prevention checks 
Completion Criteria: 
● No unauthorized access allowed 
● Session behavior matches requirements 
Integration Testing 
Objective: Ensure microservices (AuthService, BatteryService, TicketService, NotificationService, 
FileStorageService, AI Module) work together correctly, especially frontend/mobile ↔ backend 
communication. 
Technique: 
● API request/response validation 
● End-to-end data flow testing 
● Multi-service workflow testing 
Completion Criteria: 
● All API calls return correct data 
● All workflows function end-to-end without breaking 
System Testing 
Objective: Evaluate the entire system as a whole to ensure all features behave correctly. 
Technique: 
● Scenario-based testing 
● End-to-end flow testing 
● Realistic user simulation (alert → ticket → resolution) 
Completion Criteria: 
● All system-level scenarios executed 
● No critical defects remain 
● Application meets all functional requirements 
Acceptance Testing 
339 
 
Objective: Validate the system meets stakeholder expectations and is ready for deployment. 
Technique: 
● Requirement validation 
● Demonstration of key flows 
● Business-centric scenario testing 
Completion Criteria: 
● All acceptance tests passed 
● Users/GVHD sign-off received 
Regression Testing 
Objective: Ensure that new changes do not break existing features. 
Technique: 
● Re-running previously passed test cases 
● Targeted regression on affected features 
Completion Criteria: 
● All previously working features still pass 
● No old bugs reappear 
2.2 Test Levels 
Unit Testing 
Objective: Verify the correctness of individual components, functions, and business logic in isolation. 
Performed by: Backend Developers (xUnit + Moq, executed via dotnet test) 
Scope/Focus: 
● Command/Query handlers (CQRS) across the 7 backend services 
● Ticket business rules & state machine (assign, escalate, resolve) 
● Validation logic and error handling inside handlers 
● Utility/helper functions (SLA calculator, priority matrix, code generator) 
● Side effects: outbox events, activity logs, entity status updates 
● AI model inference functions (SOH/RUL/anomaly) — under the AI Module scope 
Completion Criteria: 
● All critical functions have unit tests 
● Line coverage of the Application + Domain layers (which contain the CQRS handlers and business 
rules) reaches at least 70% — the measurement on 26/08/2026 was 76.7% 
● All unit tests passed (4,364/4,364, 0 FAIL) 
No high-severity defects remain. Note: The Web (ReactJS) and Mobile (React Native) applications are 
covered by automated unit tests written with Vitest — 147 tests on the Web application and 97 on 
340 
 
the Mobile application, all passing. They cover the logic that is independent of the rendered screen: 
permission and role checks, API error mapping, SLA thresholds and countdown formatting, JWT 
session decoding, notification deep links, date formatting, ambient-threshold grading and the 
periodic-maintenance scheduling rules. On top of that, FE/Mobile quality is controlled through 
type-checking (tsc), linting (ESLint) and a successful build, and end-to-end behaviour is verified at the 
System Test / Acceptance Test level. 
Integration Testing 
Objective: Ensure that modules, components, and APIs work correctly when combined. 
Performed by: Developers + QA 
Scope/Focus: 
● Frontend/Mobile ↔ API integration 
● Authentication + session management 
● Data flow between microservices 
● AI Module ↔ BatteryService integration 
● Multi-step workflows (alert → ticket → resolve) 
Completion Criteria: 
● All integrated modules function without breaking 
● API responses correctly handled by UI 
● All integration test cases passed 
● No blockers or critical defects 
System Testing 
Objective: Validate the entire application's behavior against functional and non-functional 
requirements. 
Performed by: QA/Testers 
Scope/Focus: 
● Full user flow 
● End-to-end feature testing 
● UI/UX testing 
● Data validation 
● Error handling 
● Security and access control 
● Performance baseline checks (load time, responsiveness) 
Completion Criteria: 
● All system test cases executed 
● Critical paths verified (login, alert, ticket, notification) 
● All high-severity issues resolved or accepted with justification 
341 
 
Acceptance Testing 
Objective: Confirm the system meets the acceptance criteria defined by stakeholders and is ready for 
deployment. 
Performed by: GVHD / QA 
Scope/Focus: 
● User acceptance scenarios 
● Business logic validation 
● UI clarity and usability 
● Realistic data scenarios 
● Final approval of each feature 
Completion Criteria: 
● All acceptance tests passed 
● Stakeholder (GVHD) sign-off completed 
● No critical business-blocking defects 
● Application ready for release

GSU26SE55_Unit_Test_Report.xlsx — the Functions sheet lists all 383 actual handlers 
(CommandHandler / QueryHandler / NotificationHandler), scanned directly from the source code of 
all 9 backend test projects (AuthService, BatteryService, TicketService, NotificationService, 
FileStorageService, SmsService, AuditAggregatorService). 16 functions have detailed test cases 
derived from the real code logic, totalling 147 unit test cases — namely Login, Register, RefreshToken 
(AuthService); CreateBatteryAsset, BatchIngestSensorReadings, AcknowledgeAlert (BatteryService); 
TicketCreate, TicketAutoCreateFromAlert, TicketAssign, TicketEscalateRequest, 
TicketEscalationDecision (TicketService — the core business flow and the SLA escalation flow); 
CreateNotification, NotificationBroadcast (NotificationService); UploadFile (FileStorageService); 
QueueSms (SmsService). The remaining handlers are to be documented by the team following the 
same template. 
GSU26SE55_Integration_Test_Report.xlsx — consolidates 81 automated integration test classes 
(xUnit + WebApplicationFactory + Testcontainers) from 9 backend test projects, totalling 792 tests. 
The file contains a Cover sheet, Test Statistics, a Test Cases sheet (indexing every test class together 
with its test scope and source file, each with its own test case ID such as INT-AS-01), and one 
dedicated sheet per service. Each service sheet supports up to three testing rounds so re-test results 
can be recorded. Unlike unit tests (which mock the UnitOfWork), integration tests spin up a real HTTP 
host and a real database (PostgreSQL/Redis via Testcontainers, or in-memory SQLite), verifying 
cross-module flows end-to-end.
