modules), Test Statistics (linked by formulas to each module sheet so the totals update 
automatically), and one dedicated sheet per module as listed below:

2. Installation Guides 
2.1 System Requirements 
Backend (microservices): Docker Desktop with Docker Compose is recommended so the whole stack 
starts with a single command; alternatively .NET SDK 8 to run each service individually. Minimum 8 
GB RAM. 
Frontend (web app): Node.js 18 or later, npm or pnpm, and a modern browser (Chrome or Edge). 
Mobile app (Sonari): Node.js 18 or later, Expo CLI, and either the Expo Go app or an Android 
emulator / iOS simulator for a development build. 
AI Module: Python 3.11 (required - torch 2.6.0 is pinned to this version in the project) and pip. No 
GPU needed. 
348 
 
IoT Simulator (optional, for demo and testing): Python 3.x and make. No physical IoT hardware 
required. 
Secrets to prepare in advance: GEMINI_API_KEY (required), DEEPSEEK_API_KEY (optional) and 
BATTERY_SENSOR_INGEST_API_KEY (internal key used by IoT devices). 
2.2 Installation Instruction 
a) Backend - the whole stack via Docker Compose 
    cd backend 
    cp .env.Docker.example .env.Docker 
    (fill in GEMINI_API_KEY, POSTGRES_PASSWORD, BATTERY_SENSOR_INGEST_API_KEY, ...) 
    docker compose --env-file .env.Docker up -d --build 
 
Service endpoints once the stack is up: API Gateway http://localhost:4001 (single entry point for the 
web and mobile clients), AuthService :4002, EmailService :4003, SmsService :4004, 
FileStorageService :4005, BatteryService :4006, TicketService :4007, NotificationService :4008, 
AuditAggregatorService :4010, AI Module Swagger http://localhost:4015/docs, 
PostgreSQL/TimescaleDB localhost:5433, Redis localhost:6380, RabbitMQ management UI 
http://localhost:15673 (guest/guest), MinIO console http://localhost:9091 
(minioadmin/minioadmin), Grafana http://localhost:3001 and Prometheus http://localhost:9094. 
 
b) Frontend - web app 
    cd frontend 
    npm install 
    npm run dev        (Vite development server on port 5173) 
    npm run build      (production build) 
Configure .env so the API base URL points at the API Gateway on http://localhost:4001. 
 
c) Mobile app (Sonari) 
    cd mobile 
    npm install 
    npx expo start 
Scan the QR code with Expo Go, or run npm run android / npm run ios for a development build. Set 
EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL to the LAN IP address of the machine hosting the backend - do not use 
localhost when testing on a physical device. When using an Android emulator, run "adb reverse 
tcp:4001 tcp:4001" so the emulator can reach the gateway. 
 
d) AI Module 
    cd ai-module 
    make setup         (creates the virtual environment and installs dependencies on Python 3.11) 
    make serve         (REST API and Swagger UI at http://localhost:8000/docs) 
    make grpc          (gRPC server on :50051 - the transport used by the backend in production) 
If no trained model is available yet, generate dummy artefacts first so the service can start for a 
demo. 
 
e) IoT Simulator (optional - used for demos and testing) 
    cd iot_simulator 
    make install 
    make mock          (terminal 1: mock backend) 
349 
 
    make demo          (terminal 2: simulator connected to the mock backend) 
    IOT_BASE_URL=http://localhost:4001 make run   (connect to the real backend instead) 
Each simulated device must first be registered through POST /api/admin/iot-devices; copy the 
returned rawApiKey into config/seed.yaml. Run "make clean-state" to reset a device to factory state 
after the database has been re-seeded. 
3. User Manual 
3.1 Overview 
The Solar Lithium-ion Battery Maintenance Management System is a platform for monitoring and 
maintaining lithium-ion batteries in solar energy installations. It combines AI-based battery health 
prediction (SOH and anomaly detection) with an ITIL 4 aligned maintenance ticket workflow. 
 
End-to-end flow: IoT sensors (ESP32-S3) stream telemetry to BatteryService, the AI Module predicts 
the state of health and detects anomalies, and when an anomaly is confirmed TicketService 
automatically raises a ticket with a priority of P1, P2 or P3 together with the matching SLA. A 
Manager then triages and assigns the ticket, a Staff member resolves it, and the Customer is notified 
through the mobile app. 
 
The system serves four roles: 
- Admin - web app (/admin): user and permission management, battery types, sites, devices and 
audit logs. 
- Manager - web app (/manager): ticket triage, staff assignment, scheduling and SLA monitoring. 
- Staff - web app (/staff) and mobile app: resolving assigned tickets and recording maintenance work. 
- Customer - mobile app "Sonari": battery monitoring, alerts and support requests. 
 
Sections 3.2 to 3.9 document the primary workflows in full detail. Sections 3.10 to 3.12 summarise 
the supporting features. Every screenshot in this manual was captured from the running system with 
live data produced by the IoT simulator and the AI Module. 
3.2 Sign in and role-based workspace 
Purpose: Every user must sign in before using the system. After authentication the user is routed to 
the workspace that matches their role. Admin and Manager accounts are web-only, Customer 
accounts are mobile-only, and Staff can use both. 
Step 1: Open the web app at http://localhost:5173 and click Sign in, or launch the Sonari mobile app. 
Step 2: Enter the email address and password, then press Log in. Google sign-in is also available. 
Step 3: If two-factor authentication is enabled, enter the six-digit code from the authenticator app, 
an SMS code, or a backup code. The verification session is valid for five minutes. 
Step 4: On success the system redirects to the workspace for the account role. 
350 
 
 
Figure 3.2.1 - Sign-in screen on the mobile app

Figure 3.2.2 - Credentials entered before submitting

Figure 3.2.3 - Two-factor authentication: entering the 6-digit code

3.3 [Admin] Configure battery types, alert thresholds and sites 
Purpose: Before any battery can be monitored, an Admin defines the battery types together with 
their alert thresholds, creates the sites where batteries are installed, and registers each battery asset 
against a site and a customer. 
353 
 
Step 1: Open Battery types & Alert thresholds from the sidebar. The list shows every battery type 
with its chemistry, nominal capacity, nominal voltage and maximum cycle count. 
Step 2: Click Create battery type and fill in the specification. Alert thresholds for voltage, temperature 
and state of charge are configured per type and drive the automatic alerts described in section 3.5. 
Step 3: Open Battery & Site and click Create site to register an installation location. 
Step 4: Open the site to see its battery list, environmental readings and cascade risk summary. 
Step 5: Click Add battery on the site detail page to register a battery asset. The site is pre-selected, so 
only the serial number, battery type, customer and warranty details are required.

3.4 Monitor battery health and sensor history 
Purpose: The battery detail page is the single place where live telemetry, historical charts and the AI 
assessment come together. The AI Module runs every five minutes, predicts the state of health and 
classifies the battery as Normal, Degrading or Failed. 
Step 1: Open a site, then select the battery to inspect. The header shows the current state of health, 
the charge state and the cascade risk level. 
Step 2: The live telemetry card on the right streams voltage, current, temperature and state of charge 
as the readings arrive. 
Step 3: Use the Chart and Sensor history tabs to review how each measurement evolved over time. 
Step 4: Open the AI prediction tab to see the predicted state of health against the 80 percent 
end-of-life line, the anomaly score and the confidence of the model. 
Step 5: In the AI classification history, confirm or correct each classification using AI correct, False 
alarm or Missed. This feedback is stored and used to retrain the anomaly model.

3.5 Handle battery alerts 
Purpose: When a measurement crosses a configured threshold the system raises an alert. Alerts are 
the trigger for the automatic tickets described in section 3.7, and they are acknowledged and 
resolved from a single screen shared by Admin, Manager and Staff. 
Step 1: Open Battery alerts from the sidebar. Filter by severity (Critical, Warning, Info) or by status 
(Open, Acknowledged, Resolved). 
Step 2: Click an alert to open its detail dialog, which shows the measured value against the threshold, 
the affected battery and the linked ticket if one was created. 
Step 3: Press Acknowledge to record that the team has picked up the alert. The button is enabled 
only while the alert is still Open. 
Step 4: Press Mark resolved once the underlying problem has been fixed. 
360 
 
 
Figure 3.5.1 - Battery alert list 
 
Figure 3.5.2 - Alert detail with Acknowledge and Mark resolved enabled 
361 
 
 
Figure 3.5.3 - Alert acknowledged, ready to be marked resolved 
 
Figure 3.5.4 - Alert resolved with the full acknowledge and resolve timestamps 
362 
 
3.6 Ticket lifecycle and SLA rules 
Purpose: This section is the reference for the rest of the manual. It describes the canonical ticket 
states, how the SLA timer behaves in each state, and the rules for scheduling and customer feedback. 
Standard flow: Open, then assignment with a schedule moves the ticket to InProgress or Pending, 
the staff member reports completion which moves it to Completed, and the manager approves it 
into Closed. 
Dispatch branches: InProgress can be put on hold into Pending/Held and resumed back into 
InProgress; InProgress can raise an escalation request into Request, which the manager approves into 
ReAssign. 
 
Canonical states (TicketStatusEnum): Open (1) waiting for assignment, SLA not running. Pending (2) 
scheduled for a future start or on hold, SLA not running. InProgress (3) actively being worked on, SLA 
running for P3, P2 and P1. Request (4) escalation awaiting a decision, SLA paused. ReAssign (5) needs 
a new handler, SLA not running. Completed (6) awaiting manager approval, SLA stopped. Closed (7) 
approved and finished. ClosedRejected (8) rejected while still Open. 
 
SLA targets: P1 Critical four hours, P2 High twenty-four hours, P3 Standard seventy-two hours. Urgent 
is the highest level and has no SLA timer. On a breach the system raises the priority by exactly one 
level and moves the ticket to ReAssign; a breach from P1 to Urgent opens the incident procedure. 
 
Scheduling: a start time between five minutes ago and now activates the ticket immediately; a future 
start time places it in Pending/Scheduled. After a ticket is Closed the customer has seven days to rate 
it or reopen it. 
3.7 [Customer] Monitor batteries and raise a support request 
Purpose: Customers use the Sonari mobile app to follow the condition of the batteries they own, to 
receive alerts, and to raise a support request when something goes wrong. 
Step 1: After signing in, the dashboard shows the average charge, the number of batteries and a live 
chart that can be switched between voltage, current and temperature. 
Step 2: Tap a battery to open its detail screen with the state of health, cycle count, live 
measurements and the cascade risk badge. 
Step 3: Scroll down to the charts to review the sensor history and the charge/discharge peaks. 
Step 4: Open the Tickets tab to see all requests with the filters All, Open and Closed. Tickets prefixed 
with [Auto] were raised automatically by the system from a battery alert. 
Step 5: Tap the plus button to start a new request and follow the four steps: select the affected 
battery, choose the issue type and describe it (at least five characters), attach photos if useful, then 
review and submit. The priority is set later by the support team, not by the customer. 
Step 6: The confirmation screen shows the ticket code. Open the ticket to follow its progress through 
the six stages and to talk to the support team in the Chat tab. 
Step 7: The Alerts & Incidents screen lists every alert raised on the customer batteries. 
363

3.8 [Manager] Triage, prioritise and assign tickets 
Purpose: New tickets arrive in the manager queue, whether they were raised by a customer or 
generated automatically from a battery alert. The manager confirms the priority, chooses a handler 
whose skill tier matches that priority, and sets the start time. 
Step 1: Open Queue to see the tickets awaiting a decision, filtered by priority or category. 
Step 2: Open a ticket. The header shows the priority, the category, the SLA target and the AI 
verification result for customer-raised tickets. 
Step 3: Click Assign Staff. The dialog suggests the best handler with a match score computed from the 
skill tier and the current workload. Staff whose tier is below the requirement are disabled - a P1 
Critical ticket requires a Senior Specialist (tier 3). 
Step 4: Select the primary handler, optionally add supporters, confirm the priority and set the start 
time, then submit. A start time within the last five minutes activates the ticket immediately. 
Step 5: The ticket moves to In progress and the SLA countdown starts. 
Other actions available on an Open ticket: Change priority, Reject ticket and Merge ticket. 
374 
 
 
Figure 3.8.1 - Manager queue 
 
Figure 3.8.2 - Ticket detail before assignment 
375 
 
 
Figure 3.8.3 - Assignment dialog with the AI staff suggestion 
 
Figure 3.8.4 - Handler selected, tier requirement enforced 
376 
 
 
Figure 3.8.5 - Ticket assigned and the SLA timer running 
3.9 [Staff] Resolve a ticket, record the work, hold and escalate 
Purpose: Staff members work their assigned tickets from either the web app or the mobile app. Both 
clients expose the same actions: record maintenance work, put the ticket on hold, request an 
escalation and report the resolution. 
Step 1: Open My tickets to see the assigned work with the remaining SLA time on each card. 
Step 2: Open a ticket. The header shows the priority, the SLA countdown and the elapsed processing 
time, and the tabs give access to the conversation, the timeline, the maintenance logs and the guide 
articles. 
Step 3: Use the chat to talk to the customer. Public messages are visible to the customer while 
internal notes stay within the team. The AI assistant can draft a reply (Suggest) or condense a long 
thread (Summarize); every suggestion must be reviewed before it is sent. 
Step 4: Record the work with Add maintenance log. Choose the work type (Remote support, On-site, 
Part replacement or Inspection), describe what was done, enter the duration and attach before and 
after photos. Saved entries also appear on the ticket timeline. 
Step 5: The Guide tab recommends relevant guide articles with a match score and the reason for the 
match, and lets the technician attach an article to the ticket. 
Step 6: If the work cannot continue, press Hold and select Customer unavailable or Work blocked, 
add a note and set the next appointment. The SLA timer pauses while the ticket is on hold. 
Step 7: If the ticket exceeds the current skill tier, press Escalate and choose a reason - beyond 
handling capability, requires special equipment or parts, safety concern, or customer complaint. The 
manager then reviews the request and reassigns the ticket to a higher tier. 
Step 8: When the work is finished press Complete (Report resolution on the web app) and 
summarise the outcome. The ticket moves to Completed and waits for the manager to approve or 
reject it. 
377

3.10 Automatic tickets and AI verification (summary) 
Purpose: Not every ticket is raised by a customer. The system creates tickets on its own and uses AI 
to check the ones that customers submit. 
Ticket sources (TicketOriginEnum): ManualByCustomer (1) raised from the mobile or web app; 
AutoFromAlert (2) generated from a severe battery alert with a priority derived from the alert level; 
System (4) generated from a high cascade risk or an environmental incident, which may cover a 
whole site rather than one battery. 
 
AI verification (TicketVerifyStatusEnum): customer-raised tickets are checked automatically and 
marked Pending, Legitimate, Suspicious or Skipped when the AI service is unavailable. A manager can 
request a re-check, and the system warns when a ticket looks like a duplicate of an existing one. 
3.11 Guide articles and customer blog (summary) 
Purpose: The guide holds the technical documentation the support team uses while resolving tickets, 
and it is also the source of the public articles published for customers. 
Articles follow a four-state lifecycle (KbArticleStatusEnum): Draft, PendingReview, Published and 
Archived. A manager or admin compares the versions side by side before approving, rejecting with a 
388 
 
reason, or rolling back to an earlier version. Technicians attach the articles they consulted to the 
ticket. Customers never read the internal articles directly; instead a manager uses the AI generation 
action to turn a published article into a customer-facing blog post, reviews the draft and publishes it. 
3.12 Dashboards, SLA monitoring and reports (summary) 
Purpose: The system provides two complementary views of the operation: the overview page for the 
current situation and the analytics page for trends over time. 
The overview page shows the number of open tickets, the tickets waiting for assignment or approval, 
the SLA breach count, the on-time rate and the staff availability, together with the ticket flow, the 
priority distribution and the manager queue. It answers the question of what needs attention today. 
 
The analytics page is filtered by site, date range and granularity (day, week or month) and provides 
seven report groups: health by battery type, alert volume, top anomalies, asset lifecycle, warranties 
about to expire, environmental incidents and environmental trends. Reports export to CSV or XLSX. 
Staff members also have a dedicated SLA monitor listing the deadlines of their own tickets. 
 
VII. Conclusion 
1. Achieved Results 
The team delivered the Solar Lithium-ion Battery Maintenance Management System as a running 
platform rather than a prototype. The backend is composed of eight independently releasable 
ASP.NET Core services behind a single API gateway, each owning its own PostgreSQL schema, with 
telemetry stored in TimescaleDB hypertables, cached lookups in Redis, binary attachments in MinIO 
and asynchronous integration over RabbitMQ. Two client applications were completed — a React 19 
web application for Admin, Manager and Staff, and a React Native (Expo) mobile application for 
Customers and field Staff — together with a FastAPI + PyTorch inference service for State-of-Health 
prediction and anomaly classification, and an ESP32-S3 simulator that exercises the ingestion path 
end to end. 
In functional terms the system covers the fourteen feature groups specified in section III: 
authentication and account management, site and battery asset management, telemetry ingestion 
and IoT device management, alerts and environmental incidents, AI health prediction, ticket 
management with SLA enforcement, ticket chat, the Guide and blog, notifications, energy and fleet 
analytics, file storage, third-party data import and system administration. Cross-service consistency is 
achieved with the transactional outbox pattern and explicit sagas with compensation, rather than 
distributed transactions. 
On quality, the team wrote 4,364 unit tests across the backend services, all of which pass, reaching 
76.7% combined line coverage of the Application and Domain layers — above the 70% target set in 
section II.1.2. A further 196 manual system and acceptance test cases cover the Web and Mobile 
user interfaces and the public APIs across eight modules. 
389 
 
2. Limitations 
Several constraints remain and are stated openly. The AI module is trained on a combination of 
public battery-degradation datasets and simulated telemetry rather than on a long observation 
window of the deployed fleet, so its State-of-Health estimates should be read as decision support 
and not as a certified measurement. Coverage is uneven across services: FileStorageService sits at 
68.6%, and EmailService and ApiGateway are verified through integration tests only, without a 
line-coverage figure. The system has been validated against a simulated device fleet and a limited 
number of physical ESP32-S3 gateways, so behaviour at the scale of hundreds of concurrent sites has 
been inferred from load tests rather than observed in production. Finally, the platform is currently 
deployed on a single-region container host; high availability and disaster recovery were scoped out of 
this semester's work. 
3. Future Work 
The natural next steps follow directly from those limitations. Retraining the prediction models on 
accumulated production telemetry would replace simulated degradation curves with observed ones 
and allow the confidence interval to be narrowed. Raising coverage on FileStorageService and 
introducing measurable coverage for the gateway and the email path would close the remaining 
quality gaps. Beyond that, the team sees value in a multi-region deployment with automated failover, 
native support for additional BMS vendor protocols so that the ingestion layer is not tied to a single 
hardware family, and a customer-facing reporting API that would let operators pull energy, cost and 
carbon figures into their own business intelligence tooling. 
 
390
